Effects of Soft Coefficient
\lambda
.

We assess the impact of the soft coefficient
\lambda
on the CAR-DQN loss function by training agents in the RoadRunner environment with varying values of
\lambda
ranging from
0
to
\infty
, validating the effectiveness of soft CAR loss. When
\lambda=0
, the agent utilizes only the sample with the largest adversarial TD-error within the batch, whereas
\lambda=\infty
corresponds to averaging over all samples in the batch. It is important to note that small values of
\lambda
can lead to numerical instability. As depicted in Figure
9
, agents exhibit similar capabilities when
0.5\leq\lambda\leq 10
, indicating that the learned policies are relatively insensitive to the soft coefficient within this range. Table
4
presents the performance of CAR-DQN agents with
\lambda=0,1,\infty
across four Atari environments. In the RoadRunner,
\lambda=0
results in poor performance, achieving returns of around 25,000 due to insufficient utilization of the samples. Interestingly, utilizing only the sample with the largest adversarial TD error in the batch achieves good robustness in the other three simpler games. The case
\lambda=\infty
results in weaker robustness compared to other cases with differentiated weights. This suggests that each sample in the batch plays a distinct role in robust training, and assigning appropriate weights to the samples in a batch enhances robust performance. These results further validate the efficacy of our CAR-DQN loss function.

Additionally, we evaluate the effects of the soft coefficient
\lambda
through training CAR-PPO with various solvers and soft coefficients. As shown in Table
5
, in Hopper and Ant tasks, CAR-PPO agents show similar performance under selected coefficients and solvers, indicating that trained policies are relatively insensitive to the soft coefficient under these settings. In Walker2d and Halfcheetah environments, CAR-PPO agents are more sensitive to different
\lambda
, further confirming the effectiveness of the CAR-PPO loss function.

8
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we demonstrate the alignment of the optimal robust policy with the Bellman optimal policy within the universal Intrinsic State-adversarial MDP framework. We prove that optimizing different
k
-measurement errors yields varied performance, underscoring the necessity of infinity measurement errors for achieving adversarial robustness. Our findings are validated through experiments with CAR-DQN and CAR-PPO, which optimize surrogate objectives of infinity measurement errors. We believe this work contributes significantly to unveiling the nature of adversarial robustness in reinforcement learning.

Our theoretical focus primarily addresses the necessity of infinity measurements in the worst-case scenario. In future work, we plan to explore how DRL agents converge to vulnerable models from the perspective of feature learning theory.
Additionally, the CAR operator we introduced to characterize the adversarial robust training involves a bilevel optimization problem, and robust training itself can be framed as a minimax problem. We aim to investigate these aspects further, leveraging bilevel and minimax optimization theories and techniques to improve the efficiency of robust training.

10
,
11
,
12
, we show some examples in 3 Atari games (Pong, Freeway, and RoadRunner), indicating that the state observation
s
and adversarial observation
s_{\nu}
share the same intrinsic state.

Appendix B
Theorems and Proofs of Optimal Adversarial Robustness

B.1
Characterization of the Sparse Difference Between Intrinsic and Standard State Neighborhoods

Theorem 23
.

For any MDP
\mathcal{M}
, let
\mathcal{S}_{nu}
denote the state set where the optimal action is not unique, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{nu}=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}|\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)\text{ is not a singleton}\right\}
. Then we have the following conclusions:

•

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{s\in S|\exists\ a\in A,\text{s.t. }Q^{*}(s,a)\ \text{is not continuous at}\ s\}
, then for all
s\in\left(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{nu}\right)\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
B_{\epsilon}(s)=B^{*}_{\epsilon}(s)
.

•

Given
\epsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{S}_{nin}
denote the set of states where the intrinsic state
\epsilon
-neighourhood is not the same as the
\epsilon
-neighourhood, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{nin}=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}|B_{\epsilon}(s)\neq B^{*}_{\epsilon}(s)\right\}
. Then, we have
\mathcal{S}_{nin}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{nu}\cup\mathcal{S}_{0}+B_{\epsilon}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is the set of discontinuous points that cause the optimal action to change, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{s\in S|\forall\ \epsilon_{1}>0,\exists\ s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon_{1}}(s),\text{s.t. }\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a)\neq\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)\}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(1) Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{s\in\mathcal{S}|\exists a\in\mathcal{A},\text{s.t. $Q^{*}(s,a)$ is not continuous at }s\}
, then we have that
Q^{*}(s,a)
is continuous in
\mathcal{S}\setminus\left(\mathcal{S}_{nu}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\right)
.

Because
\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)
is a singleton for
\mathcal{S}\setminus\left(\mathcal{S}_{nu}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\right)
, we can define
\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)=\{a_{s}^{*}\}
for any
s\in\left(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{nu}\right)\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Then, we have that

Q^{*}(s,a_{s}^{*})>Q^{*}(s,a)\text{ for a fixed }a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a_{s}^{*}\}.

According to continuity of
Q^{*}(\cdot,a)
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
there exists
\epsilon_{a}>0
, such that

Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a_{s}^{*})>Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a)\text{ for all }s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon_{a}}(s).

Because
\mathcal{A}
is a finite discrete set, let
\epsilon=\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus{a_{s}^{*}}}\{\epsilon_{a}\}
, then we have that

Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime}}^{*})>Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a)\qquad\forall s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)\text{ and }a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a_{s^{\prime}}^{*}\},

which indicates that we have

B_{\epsilon}(s)=B_{\epsilon}^{*}(s).

(2) Let

\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{s\in\mathcal{S}|\forall\epsilon_{1}>0,\exists s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon_{1}}(s),\ \text{s.t. }\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a)\neq\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)\}

and

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathcal{S}_{n}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Then
S_{0}
is the set of discontinuous points that cause the optimal action to change.
And
\mu(\mathcal{S}_{0})=\mu(\mathcal{S}_{n}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=0
because
\mu(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=0
.

For any
s\in\mathcal{S}_{nin}=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}|B_{\epsilon}(s)\neq B^{*}_{\epsilon}(s)\right\}
, we have the following two cases.

Case 1
.
\exists s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)
s.t.
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{nu}
, then
s\in\mathcal{S}_{nu}+B_{\epsilon}
, i.e.

s\in\mathcal{S}_{nu}\cup\mathcal{S}_{0}+B_{\epsilon}.

Case 2
.
\forall s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon}(s),s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}_{nu}
, which means that
\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a)
is a singleton for all
s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)
. Define
\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s^{\prime},a)=\{a_{s^{\prime}}^{*}\}
for any
s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)
.

Because
s\in\mathcal{S}_{nin}
, there exist a
s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)
such that
a_{s^{\prime}}^{*}\neq a_{s}^{*}
. Let
s_{1}
be the point that closest to
s
satisfing
a_{s_{1}}^{*}\neq a_{s}^{*}
, then
s_{1}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)
. We have

s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}.

(1)

Otherwise
s_{1}\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}
means that
\exists\epsilon_{1}>0,\forall s^{\prime}\in B_{\epsilon_{1}}(s_{1})
,
a_{s^{\prime}}^{*}=a_{s_{1}}^{*}
, then
s_{1}
is not the point that closest to
s
satisfing
a_{s_{1}}^{*}\neq a_{s}^{*}
, which is a contradiction. We also have

s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}.

(2)

Otherwise
s_{1}\notin\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
means that
\forall a\in\mathcal{A}
,
Q^{*}(\cdot,a)
is continuous in
s_{1}
. First, we have

Q^{*}(s_{1},a_{s_{1}}^{*})>Q^{*}(s_{1},a),\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a_{s_{1}}^{*}\}.

Then
\exists\ \epsilon_{2}>0
,
\forall s\in B_{\epsilon_{2}}(s_{1})
, s.t.

Q^{*}(s,a_{s_{1}}^{*})>Q^{*}(s,a),\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a_{s_{1}}^{*}\}.

because of the continuity of point
s_{1}
. This contradicts the definition of
s_{1}
.

According to (
1
) and (
2
), we have

s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}
i.e.
s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
. Then
s\in\mathcal{S}_{0}+B_{\epsilon}(s)
, i.e.

s\in\mathcal{S}_{nu}\cup\mathcal{S}_{0}+B_{\epsilon}.

Thus

\mathcal{S}_{nin}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{nu}\cup\mathcal{S}_{0}+B_{\epsilon}.

Therefore, the proof of the theorem is concluded.
∎

Remark 24
.

In practical complex tasks, we can view
\mathcal{S}_{nu}
as an empty set.

Remark 25
.

Except for the smooth environment, many tasks can be modeled as environments with sparse rewards. Further, the value and action-value functions in these environments are almost everywhere continuous, indicating the set
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is a zero-measure set.

Remark 26
.

According to the above characteristics, we know that
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is a set of special discontinuous points and its elements are rare in practical complex environments.

Further, we can get the following corollary in the setting of continuous functions and there are better conclusions.

Corollary 27
.

For any MDP
\mathcal{M}
, let
\mathcal{S}_{nu}
denote the state set where the optimal action is not unique, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{nu}=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}|\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)\text{ is not a singleton}\right\}
. If
Q^{*}(\cdot,a)
is continuous in
\mathcal{S}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have the following conclusions:

•

For
s\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{nu}
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
B_{\epsilon}(s)=B^{*}_{\epsilon}(s)
.

•

Given
\epsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{S}_{nin}
denote the set of states where the intrinsic state
\epsilon
-neighourhood is not the same as the
\epsilon
-neighourhood, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{nin}=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}|B_{\epsilon}(s)\neq B^{*}_{\epsilon}(s)\right\}
. Then, we have
\mathcal{S}_{nin}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{nu}+B_{\epsilon}=\left\{s_{1}+s_{2}|s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{nu},\ \|s_{2}\|\leq\epsilon\right\}
. Especially, when
\mathcal{S}_{nu}
is a finite set, we have
\mu\left(\mathcal{S}_{nin}\right)\leq\left|\mathcal{S}_{nu}\right|\mu\left(B_{\epsilon}\right)=C_{d}\left|\mathcal{S}_{nu}\right|\epsilon^{d}
, where
C_{d}
is a constant with respect to dimension
d
and norm.

Proof.

Corollary
27
can be derived from Theorem
23

because we have the following conclusion in continuous case.

\mathcal{S}_{0}\subseteq\{s\in\mathcal{S}|\exists a\in\mathcal{A},\text{s.t. $Q^{*}(s,a)$ is not continuous at }s\}=\emptyset.

Therefore, the theorem is concluded.
∎

Remark 28
.

Certain natural environments show smooth reward function and transition dynamics, especially in continuous control tasks where the transition dynamics come from some physical laws. Further, the value and action-value functions in these environments are continuous, making
\mathcal{S}_{0}
an empty set.

B.2
Consistent Optimal Robust Policy and CAR Operator

Define the consistent adversarial robust operator for adversarial action-value function:

\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q\right)(s,a)=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right].

B.2.1
CAR Operator is Not a Contraction

Theorem 29
.

\mathcal{T}_{car}
is not a contraction.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}=[-1,1]
,
\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
,
0<\epsilon\ll 1
and dynamic transition
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)
be a determined function. Let
n>\max\{\frac{\delta}{\gamma},2\delta\}
,
\delta>0
and

\displaystyle Q_{1}(s,a_{1})

\displaystyle=2n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[-1,0\right)\right\}}+\left[2n-\frac{2n-2\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}s\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[0,\frac{1}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+2\delta\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{8}\epsilon,\frac{3}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}

\displaystyle+\left[2\delta+\frac{n-2\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}\left(s-\frac{3\epsilon}{8}\right)\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{3}{8}\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon,1\right]\right\}},

\displaystyle Q_{1}(s,a_{2})

\displaystyle=n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[-1,0\right)\right\}}+\left[n-\frac{n-\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}s\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[0,\frac{1}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+\delta\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{8}\epsilon,\frac{3}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}

\displaystyle+\left[\delta+\frac{2n-\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}\left(s-\frac{3\epsilon}{8}\right)\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{3}{8}\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+2n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon,1\right]\right\}},

\displaystyle Q_{2}(s,a_{1})

\displaystyle=2n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[-1,0\right)\right\}}+\left[2n-\frac{2n-\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}s\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[0,\frac{1}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+\delta\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{8}\epsilon,\frac{3}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}

\displaystyle+\left[\delta+\frac{n-\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}\left(s-\frac{3\epsilon}{8}\right)\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{3}{8}\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon,1\right]\right\}},

\displaystyle Q_{2}(s,a_{2})

\displaystyle=n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[-1,0\right)\right\}}+\left[n-\frac{n-2\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}s\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[0,\frac{1}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+2\delta\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{8}\epsilon,\frac{3}{8}\epsilon\right)\right\}}

\displaystyle+\left[2\delta+\frac{2n-2\delta}{\frac{1}{8}\epsilon}\left(s-\frac{3\epsilon}{8}\right)\right]\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{3}{8}\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\right)\right\}}+2n\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s\in\left[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon,1\right]\right\}}.

Then

\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\delta.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{1}(s,a)-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{2}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{1}_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime})}Q_{1}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{1}_{\nu}}}Q_{1}\left(s^{1}_{\nu},a_{s^{1}_{\nu}}\right)\right)-\right.

\displaystyle\left.\min_{s^{2}_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime})}Q_{2}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{2}_{\nu}}}Q_{2}\left(s^{2}_{\nu},a_{s^{2}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right].

Let
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}=-\frac{\epsilon}{2}|s,a)=1
and
s^{\prime}=-\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, then

\displaystyle\min_{s^{1}_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime})}Q_{1}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{1}_{\nu}}}Q_{1}\left(s^{1}_{\nu},a_{s^{1}_{\nu}}\right)\right)=Q_{1}(s^{\prime},a_{1}),

\displaystyle\min_{s^{1}_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime})}Q_{2}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{2}_{\nu}}}Q_{2}\left(s^{2}_{\nu},a_{s^{2}_{\nu}}\right)\right)=Q_{2}(s^{\prime},a_{2}).

Thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{1}(s,a)-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{2}(s,a)=\gamma\left[Q_{1}(s^{\prime},a_{1})-Q_{2}(s^{\prime},a_{2})\right]=\gamma n>\delta,

which means that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{1}-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}>\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{T}_{car}
is not a contraction.
∎

B.2.2
Fixed Point of CAR Operator

Lemma 30

(Bellman equations for fixed
\pi
and
\nu
in SA-MDP,
Zhang et al. 2020b
)
.

Given
\pi:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})
and
\nu:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle V^{\pi\circ\nu}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s\right)}\right)}\left[r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}V^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime})\right],

\displaystyle Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}V^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a),a^{\prime}\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}).

Lemma 31

(Bellman equation for strongest adversary
\nu^{*}
in SA-MDP,
Zhang et al. 2020b
)
.

V^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s)=\min_{\nu(s)\in B(s)}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s,a).

Definition 32
.

Define the linear functional
\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}:L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\rightarrow L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
for fixed
\pi
and
\nu
:

\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q\right)(s,a):=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a),a^{\prime}\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right)}Q(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}).

Then, by lemma
30
, we have that

Q^{\pi\circ\nu}=r+\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}.

Lemma 33
.

\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a linear functional where
\mathcal{X}
are normed vector space. If there exists
m>0
such that

\|\mathcal{T}x\|\geq m\|x\|\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

then
\mathcal{T}
has a bounded inverse operator
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{T}x_{1}=\mathcal{T}x_{2}
, then
\mathcal{T}(x_{1}-x_{2})=0
. While
0=\|\mathcal{T}(x_{1}-x_{2})\|\geq m\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|
, thus
x_{1}=x_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{T}
is a bijection and thus the inverse operator of
\mathcal{T}
exists.

For any
y\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{T}^{-1}y\in\mathcal{X}
. We have that

\displaystyle\|y\|=\|\mathcal{T}\left(\mathcal{T}^{-1}y\right)\|\geq m\|\mathcal{T}^{-1}y\|.

Thus, we attain that

\|\mathcal{T}^{-1}y\|\leq\frac{1}{m}\|y\|,\quad\forall y\in\mathcal{X},

which shows that
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
is bounded.
∎

Lemma 34
.

I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}
is invertible and thus we have that

Q^{\pi\circ\nu}=\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{-1}r.

Proof.

Firstly, for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a),a^{\prime}\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right)}Q(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a),a^{\prime}\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right)}\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

Thus, we have that

\left\|\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

(3)

For any
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left\|Q-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}-\gamma\left\|\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}-\gamma\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\gamma\right)\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the first inequality comes from the triangle inequality and the second inequality comes from (
3
). Then, according to lemma
33
, we attain that
I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}
is invertible.
∎

Lemma 35
.

If
Q>0
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, then we have that
\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{-1}Q>0
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

At first, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)\left(\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{t}-\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=I.

Thus, we get that

\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{-1}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{t}.

If
Q(s,a)>0
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, then for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q\right)(s,a)=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a),a^{\prime}\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right)}Q(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\geq 0.

Further, we have that
\left(\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{k}Q\right)(s,a)>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{-1}Q(s,a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\left(\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{t}Q\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle>0.

Therefore, the proof of the lemma is concluded.
∎

Theorem 36
.

If the optimal adversarial action-value function under the strongest adversary
Q_{0}(s,a):=\max_{\pi}\min_{\nu}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)
exists for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, then it is the fixed point of CAR operator.

Proof.

Denote
V_{0}(s)=\max_{\pi}\min_{\nu}V^{\pi\circ\nu}(s)
.
For all
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{0}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\max_{\pi}\min_{\nu}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\max_{\pi}\min_{\nu}\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}V^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}V_{0}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\min_{\nu(s)\in B(s)}\max_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu\left(s\right)}\right)}Q_{0}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q_{0}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q_{0}\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q\right)(s,a),

where the fourth equation comes from lemma
31
. This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 37
.

Within the ISA-MDP,
Q^{*}
is the fixed point of the CAR operator. Further,
Q^{*}
is the optimal adversarial action-value function under the strongest adversary, i.e.
Q^{*}(s,a)=\max_{\pi}\min_{\nu}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)
, for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}\max_{a^{\prime}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=Q^{*}(s,a),

where the second equality utilizes the definition of
B^{*}(s^{\prime})
. Thus,
Q^{*}
is a fixed point of the CAR operator.

Define
\pi
and
\nu
as the following:

\displaystyle\pi(s)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q^{*}(s,a),

(4)

\displaystyle\nu(s)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\arg\min}_{s_{\nu}\in B(s)}Q^{*}\left(s,\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s_{\nu}}}Q^{*}\left(s_{\nu},a_{s_{\nu}}\right)\right).

(5)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{\nu(s^{\prime})}}Q^{*}\left(\nu(s^{\prime}),a_{\nu(s^{\prime})}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\pi\left(\nu(s^{\prime})\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}Q^{*}\right)(s,a).

Thus, we have

Q^{*}=\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{-1}r=Q^{\pi\circ\nu},

where equations comes from lemma
34
. Further, according to the definition of ISA-MDP, we attain
Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)=Q^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}
.
This shows that
Q^{*}
is the action-value adversarial function of policy
\pi
under the strongest adversary
\nu=\nu^{*}(\pi)
.

According to the definition of
B^{*}
, within the ISA-MDP, we have that

\pi(\nu(s))=\pi(s),\quad\forall s\in\mathcal{S}.

(6)

Then, for any stationary policy
\pi^{\prime}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left[\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}-\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)Q^{\pi\circ\nu}\right](s,a)

(7)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{\pi\circ\nu}\left(s^{\prime},\pi\left(\nu(s^{\prime})\right)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{a^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\prime}\left(\cdot|{\nu^{*}\left(s^{\prime};\pi^{\prime}\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{\pi\circ\nu}\left(s^{\prime},\pi\left(s^{\prime}\right)\right)-\mathbb{E}_{a^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\prime}\left(\cdot|{\nu^{*}\left(s^{\prime};\pi^{\prime}\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a),a^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\prime}\left(\cdot|{\nu^{*}\left(s^{\prime};\pi^{\prime}\right)}\right)}\left[Q^{\pi\circ\nu}\left(s^{\prime},\pi\left(s^{\prime}\right)\right)-Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\geq 0,

where the second equality comes from (
6
) and the last inequality comes from (
4
).

Further, we have that

\displaystyle Q^{*}-Q^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=Q^{\pi\circ\nu}-Q^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=Q^{\pi\circ\nu}-\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)^{-1}r

\displaystyle=Q^{\pi\circ\nu}-\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)^{-1}\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)Q^{\pi\circ\nu}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)^{-1}\left(\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)-\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)\right)Q^{\pi\circ\nu}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}-\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}\right)Q^{\pi\circ\nu}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

where the last inequality comes from (
7
) and lemma
35
.

Thus, we have that
Q^{\pi\circ\nu}=Q^{*}\geq Q^{\pi^{\prime}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{\prime})}
for all policy
\pi^{\prime}
which shows that
\pi
is the optimal robust policy under strongest adversary.
∎

Corollary 38
.

Within the ISA-MDP, there exists a deterministic and stationary policy
\pi^{*}
which satisfies
V^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}(s)\geq V^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s)
and
Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}(s,a)\geq Q^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s,a)
for all
\pi\in\Pi
,
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

According to theorem
37
, we have that
Q^{*}(s,a)=\max_{\pi}\min_{\nu}Q^{\pi\circ\nu}(s,a)
, for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
Define
\pi^{*}
and
\nu^{*}
as the following:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(s)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q^{*}(s,a),

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(s)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\arg\min}_{s_{\nu}\in B(s)}Q^{*}\left(s,\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s_{\nu}}}Q^{*}\left(s_{\nu},a_{s_{\nu}}\right)\right).

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{\nu(s^{\prime})}}Q^{*}\left(\nu^{*}(s^{\prime}),a_{\nu(s^{\prime})}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},\pi^{*}\left(\nu^{*}(s^{\prime})\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\left(\mathcal{L}^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}}Q^{*}\right)(s,a).

Thus, we have

Q^{*}=\left(I-\gamma\mathcal{L}^{\pi\circ\nu}\right)^{-1}r=Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}},

where equations comes from lemma
34
. Further, according to the definition of ISA-MDP, we attain
Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}}(s,a)=Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}
.
This shows that
Q^{*}
is the action-value adversarial function of policy
\pi^{*}
under the strongest adversary
\nu*=\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})
. Thus, we have that

Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}(s,a)\geq Q^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s,a),\quad\forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}.

(8)

For any policy
\pi
and
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we have that

\displaystyle V^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi^{*}\left(\cdot|{\nu^{*}\left(s;\pi^{*}\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\max_{a}Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}(\pi^{*})}(s,a)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu^{*}\left(s;\pi\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi^{*}\circ\nu^{*}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi\left(\cdot|{\nu^{*}\left(s;\pi\right)}\right)}Q^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s,a)

\displaystyle=V^{\pi\circ\nu^{*}(\pi)}(s),

where the first and last equations come from lemma
30
and the last inequality comes from (
8
). Therefore, the proof of the corollary is concluded.
∎

B.2.3
Convergence of CAR operator

In this section, we prove a conclusion for convergence of the fixed point iterations of the CAR operator under the
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth environment assumption.

Definition 39

(
Bukharin et al. 2024
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We say the environment is
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth, if the reward function
r:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and the transition dynamics
\mathbb{P}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\Delta\left(\mathcal{S}\right)
satisfy

\left|r(s,a)-r\left(s^{\prime},a\right)\right|\leq L_{r}\left\|s-s^{\prime}\right\|\text{ and }\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)-\mathbb{P}\left(\cdot\mid s^{\prime},a\right)\right\|_{L^{1}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\leq L_{\mathbb{P}}\left\|s-s^{\prime}\right\|,

for
\left(s,s^{\prime},a\right)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
.
\|\cdot\|
denotes a metric on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The definition is motivated by observations that certain natural environments show smooth reward function and transition dynamics, especially in continuous control tasks where the transition dynamics come from some physical laws.

The following lemma shows that
\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q
is uniformly bounded.

Lemma 40
.

Suppose
Q
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r}\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(\cdot,a)
is uniformly bounded, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s,a)\right|\leq C_{Q},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A},

where
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
. Further, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q(\cdot,a)
has the same uniform bound as
\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(\cdot,a)
, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q(s,a)\right|\leq C_{Q},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}.

(9)

Proof.

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|r(s,a)\right|+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left|\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq M_{r}+\gamma M_{Q}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\},\qquad\forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}.

Let
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
. Suppose the inequality (
9
) holds for
k=n
. Then, for
k=n+1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{n+1}Q(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}\mathcal{T}_{car}^{n}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}\mathcal{T}_{car}^{n}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|r(s,a)\right|+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left|\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}\mathcal{T}_{car}^{n}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}\mathcal{T}_{car}^{n}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq M_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma)C_{Q}+\gamma C_{Q}

\displaystyle=C_{Q}.

By induction, we have
\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q(s,a)\right|\leq C_{Q},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A},k\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

The following lemma shows that
\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q
is uniformly Lipschitz continuous in the
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth environment.

Lemma 41
.

Suppose the environment is
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth and suppose
Q
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r}\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(\cdot,a)
is Lipschitz continuous, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s,a)-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s^{\prime},a)\right|\leq L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\|s-s^{\prime}\|,

where
L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}=L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}
and
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
. Further, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q(\cdot,a)
is Lipschitz continuous and has the same Lipschitz constant as
\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(\cdot,a)
, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q(s,a)-\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q(s^{\prime},a)\right|\leq L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\|s-s^{\prime}\|.

Proof.

For all
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s_{1},a)-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s_{2},a)

\displaystyle=r(s_{1},a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s_{1},a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad-r(s_{2},a)-\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s_{2},a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(r(s_{1},a)-r(s_{2},a)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right)\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)ds^{\prime}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s_{1},a)-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q(s_{2},a)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left(r(s_{1},a)-r(s_{2},a)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right)\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)ds^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\leq L_{r}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\left|\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right|\left|\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)\right)\right|ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq L_{r}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|+\gamma C_{Q}\int_{s^{\prime}}\left|\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right|ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq L_{r}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|

\displaystyle=\left(L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|.

The second inequality comes from the Lipschitz property of
r
. The third inequality comes from the uniform boundedness of
Q
and the last inequality utilizes the Lipschitz property of
\mathbb{P}
.

Note that
\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}
and
\mathcal{T}_{car}
have the same uniform boundedness
C_{Q}
. Then, due to lemma
40
, we can extend the above proof to
\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}
.
∎

Remark 42
.

Note that if replace the operator
\mathcal{T}_{car}
in the Lemma
40
and Lemma
41
with Bellman optimality operator
\mathcal{T}_{B}
, these lemmas still hold.

The following lemma shows that the fixed point iteration has a property close to contraction.

Lemma 43
.

Suppose
Q
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r}\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Let
Q^{*}
denote the Bellman optimality Q-function. Within the ISA-MDP, we have

\|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma\left(\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q\left(s,a\right)-Q\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|\right).

Let
\epsilon
denote the diameter of
B
, i.e.,
\epsilon=\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{s_{1},s_{2}\in B(s)}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|
.
Further, if
Q(\cdot,a)
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous with respect to
s\in\mathcal{S}
, i.e

\left|Q(s,a)-Q(s^{\prime},a)\right|\leq L\|s-s^{\prime}\|,\quad\forall s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\ a\in\mathcal{A},

we have

\|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\gamma L\epsilon.

Proof.

Denote
a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu},Q}^{*}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a\right)
and
s^{\prime,*}_{\nu}=\arg\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)
. If
\mathcal{T}_{car}Q>\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q\right)(s,a)-\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)-\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)-Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)-Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)+Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)-Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)-Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}}\left(Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)-Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty},

where the second equality utilize the definition of
B^{*}(s^{\prime})
and the first inequality comes from the optimality of
a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}
.
If
\mathcal{T}_{car}Q<\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\right)(s,a)-\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-\min_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

(10)

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

(11)

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)\right].

(12)

We will separately analyze the items
10
,
11
and
12
. Firstly, we can bound the item
10
with
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}
.

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}}\left(Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)-Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}.

For the item
11
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}}\left(Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a^{\prime}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}\max_{a^{\prime}}\left|Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q\left(s,a\right)-Q\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|.

Due to
a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a\right)
, we have
Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a\right)\leq Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right),\ \forall a
. Then, for the item
12
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime},Q^{*}}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime},a_{s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},Q}^{*}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}}\left|Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime,*}_{\nu},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{s^{\prime}_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s^{\prime})}\max_{a^{\prime}}\left|Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)-Q\left(s^{\prime}_{\nu},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q\left(s,a\right)-Q\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\right)(s,a)-\left(\mathcal{T}_{car}Q\right)(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left(\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q\left(s,a\right)-Q\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|\right).

In a summary, we get

\|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma\left(\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q\left(s,a\right)-Q\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|\right).

Further, when
Q(\cdot,a)
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous, i.e

\left|Q(s,a)-Q(s^{\prime},a)\right|\leq L\|s-s^{\prime}\|,\quad\forall s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\ a\in\mathcal{A},

we have

\displaystyle\quad\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q\left(s,a\right)-Q\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}L\|s-s_{\nu}\|

\displaystyle\leq L\epsilon.

Then, we have

\|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma\left(\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2L\epsilon\right).

Therefore, the proof of the lemma is concluded.
∎

Remark 44
.

We can relax the Lipschitz condition to local Lipschitz continuous in the
B^{*}(s)
.

We prove that the fixed point iterations of
\mathcal{T}_{car}
at least converge to a sub-optimal solution close to
Q^{*}
in the
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth environment.

Theorem 45
.

Suppose the environment is
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth and suppose
Q_{0}
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q_{0}},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q_{0}(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q_{0}},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Let
Q^{*}
denote the Bellman optimal Q-function and
Q_{k+1}=\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{k}=\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k+1}Q_{0}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\epsilon
denote the diameter of
B
, i.e.,
\epsilon=\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{s_{1},s_{2}\in B(s)}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|
. Within the ISA-MDP, we have that

\|Q_{k+1}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma^{k+1}\|Q_{0}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+\gamma^{k+1}D_{Q_{0}}+\frac{2\gamma\epsilon}{1-\gamma}L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}},

where
D_{Q_{0}}=2\max_{s,a}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q_{0}\left(s,a\right)-Q_{0}\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|
is a constant relating to the local continuity of initial
Q_{0}
,
L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}=L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q_{0}}L_{\mathbb{P}}
and
C_{Q_{0}}=\max\left\{M_{Q_{0}},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
.

Proof.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\|Q_{k+1}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k+1}Q_{0}-\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k+1}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q_{0}-\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\gamma L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left(\gamma\|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k-1}Q_{0}-\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k-1}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\gamma L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\epsilon\right)+2\gamma L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\epsilon

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}\|\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k-1}Q_{0}-\mathcal{T}_{car}^{k-1}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\epsilon L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\sum_{l=1}^{2}\gamma^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\cdots

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{k}\|\mathcal{T}_{car}Q_{0}-\mathcal{T}_{car}Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\epsilon L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\gamma^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{k+1}\|Q_{0}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\gamma^{k+1}\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q_{0}\left(s,a\right)-Q_{0}\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|+2\epsilon L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\gamma^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{k+1}\|Q_{0}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}+2\gamma^{k+1}\max_{s}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B^{*}(s)}\max_{a}\left|Q_{0}\left(s,a\right)-Q_{0}\left(s_{\nu},a\right)\right|+\frac{2\gamma\epsilon}{1-\gamma}L_{\mathcal{T}_{car}}.

The first and second inequalities come from Lemma
41
and Lemma
43
. The penultimate inequality comes from Lemma
43
. Therefore, the proof of the theorem is concluded.
∎

Appendix C
Theorems and Proofs of Policy Robustness under Bellman p-error in Action-value Function Space

Banach Space
is a complete normed space
\left(X,\|\cdot\|\right)
, consisting of a vector space
X
together with a norm
\|\cdot\|:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
. In this paper, we consider the setting where the continuous state space
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a compact set and the action space
\mathcal{A}
is a finite set. We discuss in the Banach space
\left(L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),\|\cdot\|_{p}\right),\ 1\leq p\leq\infty
. Define
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)=\left\{f|\|f\|_{p}<\infty\right\}
, where
\|f\|_{p}=\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|f(s,a)\right|^{p}d\mu(s)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
for
\ 1\leq p<\infty
,
\mu
is the measure over
\mathcal{S}
and
\|f\|_{\infty}=\inf\left\{M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}|\left|f(s,a)\right|\leq M\text{ for almost every }(s,a)\right\}
. For simplicity, we refer to this Banach space as
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
.

C.1
Infinity Norm Space is Necessary for Adversarial Robustness in Action-value Function Space

Theorem 46
.

There exists an MDP instance
\mathcal{M}
such that the following statements hold. Given a function
Q
and adversary perturbation budget
\epsilon
, let
\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}
denote the set of states where the greedy policy according to
Q
is suboptimal, i.e.
\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}=\left\{s|Q^{*}(s,\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q(s,a))<\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)\right\}
and let
\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}
denote the set of states in whose
\epsilon
-neighbourhood there exists the adversarial state, i.e.
\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}=\left\{s|\exists s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s),\ \text{s.t. }Q^{*}(s,\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q(s_{\nu},a))<\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)\right\}
, where
Q^{*}
is the Bellman optimal
Q
-function.

•

For any
1\leq p<\infty
and
\delta>0
, there exists a function
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
satisfying
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\delta
such that
m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}\right)=O(\delta)
yet
m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}\right)=m\left(\mathcal{S}\right)
.

•

There exists a
\bar{\delta}>0
such that for any
0<\delta\leq\bar{\delta}
, for any function
Q\in L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
satisfying
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\delta
, we have that
m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}\right)=O(\delta)
and
m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}\right)=2\epsilon+O\left(\delta\right)
.

Proof.

Given a MDP instance
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{S}=[-1,1]
,
\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and

\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{1})=\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s-\epsilon_{1}\right\}},&\quad s\in[-1+\epsilon_{1},1]\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=-1\right\}},&\quad s\in[-1,-1+\epsilon_{1})\end{aligned}\right.

\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s+\epsilon_{1}\right\}},&\quad s\in[-1,1-\epsilon_{1}]\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=1\right\}},&\quad s\in(1-\epsilon_{1},1]\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle r(s,a_{1})

\displaystyle=-ks,

\displaystyle r(s,a_{2})

\displaystyle=ks.

where
\mathbb{P}
is the transition dynamic,
r
is the reward function,
k>0
,
0<\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{\cdot\right\}}
is the indicator function. Let
\gamma
be the discount factor.

First, we prove that equation (
13
) is the optimal policy.

\pi^{*}(s)=\arg\max_{a}Q^{*}(s,a)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \{a_{2}\},s>0\\
\{a_{1}\},s<0\\
\{a_{1},a_{2}\},s=0\end{aligned}\right.

(13)

Define
s_{t}^{\pi}
is state rollouted by policy
\pi
in time step
t
.

Let
s_{0}>0
, then

•

If
s_{t}^{\pi^{*}}=1
, while
s_{t}^{\pi}\in[-1,1]
, then
s_{t}^{\pi^{*}}\geq|s_{t}^{\pi}|
hold for any policy
\pi
.

•

If
s_{t}^{\pi^{*}}<1
. First, we have

0<s_{t}^{\pi^{*}}=s_{0}+t\epsilon_{1}<1,

-1<s_{0}-t\epsilon_{1}.

Then for any policy
\pi
, we have the following equation by definition of transition,

s_{t}^{\pi}=s_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{t}x_{i}\epsilon_{1},

where
x_{i}\in\{-1,1\},i=1,...,t
. Then

s_{t}^{\pi^{*}}\geq|s_{t}^{\pi}|.

Then for any policy
\pi
,
s_{0}>0
and
t\geq 0
, we have

s_{t}^{\pi^{*}}\geq|s_{t}^{\pi}|,

and

\displaystyle\pi(a_{2}|s_{t}^{\pi})r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{2})+\pi(a_{1}|s_{t}^{\pi})r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{1})

(14)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\pi(a_{2}|s_{t}^{\pi})(ks_{t}^{\pi})+\pi(a_{1}|s_{t}^{\pi})(-ks_{t}^{\pi}),\pi(a_{2}|s_{t}^{\pi})(-ks_{t}^{\pi})+\pi(a_{1}|s_{t}^{\pi})(ks_{t}^{\pi})\}

\displaystyle=\left|ks_{t}^{\pi}\left(\pi(a_{2}|s_{t}^{\pi})-\pi(a_{1}|s_{t}^{\pi})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|ks_{t}^{\pi}\right|

\displaystyle\leq ks_{t}^{\pi^{*}}

\displaystyle=r(s_{t}^{\pi^{*}},a_{2}).

Let
s_{0}
be the initial state,
\tau=(s_{0},...)
be the trajectory of policy
\pi
. Define
J(\pi,s_{0})=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t},a_{t})
is expected reward in initial state
s_{0}
about policy
\pi
. Then

\displaystyle J(\pi^{*},s_{0})-J(\pi,s_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{t}^{\pi^{*}},a_{t}\sim\pi^{*}(\cdot|s_{t}^{\pi^{*}})}\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t}^{\pi^{*}},a_{t})-\mathbb{E}_{s_{t}^{\pi},a_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|s_{t}^{\pi})}\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t}^{\pi^{*}},a_{2})-\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}\mathbb{E}_{s_{t}^{\pi},a_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|s_{t}^{\pi})}r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t}^{\pi^{*}},a_{2})-\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}\mathbb{E}_{s_{t}^{\pi}}\left[\pi(a_{2}|s_{t}^{\pi})r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{2})+\pi(a_{1}|s_{t}^{\pi})r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}\mathbb{E}_{s_{t}^{\pi}}\left[r(s_{t}^{\pi^{*}},a_{2})-\left[\pi(a_{2}|s_{t}^{\pi})r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{2})+\pi(a_{1}|s_{t}^{\pi})r(s_{t}^{\pi},a_{1})\right]\right]

(15)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

(16)

For (
C.1
), the policy
\pi^{*}
and dynamic transition
\mathbb{P}
are deterministic. For (
16
), We use property (
14
).

Then for
s>0
, we get that the optimal policy is
\pi(\cdot|s)=a_{2}
.
By symmetry, we can also get that the optimal policy is
\pi(\cdot|s)=a_{1}
for
s<0
and
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
are also optimal action for
s=0
. Thus we have proved equation (
13
) is the optimal policy.

First,we have the following equation according to (
13
)

\displaystyle Q^{*}(0,a_{2})=Q^{*}(0,a_{1}).

(17)

For
s>0
, we have

\displaystyle Q^{*}(s,a_{2})

(18)

\displaystyle=ks+\gamma k(s+\epsilon_{1})+\gamma^{2}k(s+2\epsilon_{1})+...+\gamma^{t_{s}}k(s+t_{s}\epsilon_{1})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t_{s}+n}k\times 1

\displaystyle=ks+k\left[\sum_{t=1}^{t_{s}}\gamma^{t}\left(s+t\epsilon_{1}\right)+\sum_{t=t_{s}+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\right],

where
s+t_{s}\epsilon_{1}\in(1-\epsilon_{1},1]
, i.e.
t_{s}=\lfloor\frac{1-s}{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor
.

For
s\geq\epsilon_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle Q^{*}(s,a_{1})

(19)

\displaystyle=-ks+\gamma k(s-\epsilon_{1})+\gamma^{2}ks+\dots+\gamma^{t_{s}+2}k(s+t_{s}\epsilon_{1})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t_{s}+2+n}k\times 1

\displaystyle=-ks+k\left[\sum_{t=1}^{t_{s}+2}\gamma^{t}\left(s+(t-2)\epsilon_{1}\right)+\sum_{t=t_{s}+3}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\right].

For
0<s<\epsilon_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle Q^{*}(s,a_{1})

(20)

\displaystyle=-ks+\gamma(-k)(s-\epsilon_{1})+\gamma^{2}(-k)(s-2\epsilon_{1})+\dots+\gamma^{q_{s}}(-k)(s-q_{s}\epsilon_{1})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{q_{s}+n}(-k)(-1)

\displaystyle=-ks+k\left[\sum_{t=1}^{q_{s}}\gamma^{t}\left(t\epsilon_{1}-s\right)+\sum_{t=q_{s}+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\right].

where
s-q_{s}\epsilon_{1}\in[-1,-1+\epsilon_{1})
,i.e.
q_{s}=\lfloor\frac{1+s}{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor>t_{s}
.

According to (
18
), (
19
), (
20
) and
q_{s}>t_{s}
, we have

Q^{*}(s,a_{2})-Q^{*}(s,a_{1})>2ks,s>0.

(21)

By symmetry, we can also get

Q^{*}(s,a_{1})-Q^{*}(s,a_{2})>-2ks,s<0.

(22)

(1) First, we have

0<Q^{*}(s,a)<\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}.

(23)

For any
1\leq p<\infty
, let
n>\max\left\{\frac{1}{\epsilon},\left(\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\right)^{p},\delta^{p},\delta^{p-1}\right\}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\displaystyle Q(s,a_{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Q^{*}(s,a_{2})-n^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad s\in[\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{\delta^{p}}{n^{2}}],k=0,1,\dots,n-1\\
Q^{*}(s,a_{2}),\quad\text{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle Q(s,a_{1})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Q^{*}(s,a_{1})-n^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad s\in[-\frac{k+1}{n},-\frac{k+1}{n}+\frac{\delta^{p}}{n^{2}}],k=0,1,\dots,n-1\\
Q^{*}(s,a_{1}),\quad\text{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\quad\ \|Q(s,a_{1})-Q^{*}(s,a_{1})\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle=\|Q(s,a_{2})-Q^{*}(s,a_{2})\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left[n*\frac{\delta^{p}}{n^{2}}*\left(n^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)^{p}\right]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta.

And

\displaystyle\quad\ \|Q(s,a)\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle=\|Q(s,a)-Q^{*}(s,a)+Q^{*}(s,a)\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Q(s,a)-Q^{*}(s,a)\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}+\|Q^{*}(s,a)\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle<\infty.

which means
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
.

We have the following two inequalities because
n>\left(\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\right)^{p}
and (
23
),

Q^{*}(s,a_{2})-n^{\frac{1}{p}}<Q^{*}(s,a_{1}),

Q^{*}(s,a_{1})-n^{\frac{1}{p}}<Q^{*}(s,a_{2}).

Then, we have that

\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}=\bigcup_{k=-n}^{n-1}\left[\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{\delta^{p}}{n^{2}}\right]

(24)

and

\displaystyle m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}\right)=2n*\frac{\delta^{p}}{n^{2}}<2\delta=O\left(\delta\right)

because of
n>\delta^{p-1}
.

According to (
24
), the distance between any two adjacent intervals of
\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}
is less than
\epsilon
. For any
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
\exists\ k\in\{-n,-n+1,...,n-1\}
s.t.
s\in[\frac{k}{n},\frac{k+1}{n}]
. Because
n>\frac{1}{\epsilon}

(i.e.
\frac{1}{n}<\epsilon
), then we have that

d(s,\frac{k}{n})<\epsilon,\text{ i.e. }d(s,\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub})<\epsilon,

where
d(\cdot,\cdot)
is Euclid distance. According to the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}
, we have
\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}=\mathcal{S}
and

m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}\right)=m\left(\mathcal{S}\right).

(2) Let
\bar{\delta}\in(0,k]
, for any
0<\delta\leq\bar{\delta}
, for any state-action value function
Q\in L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
satisfying
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\delta
, we can get the following two inequalities by (
21
) and (
22
).

Q(s,a_{2})\geq Q^{*}(s,a_{2})-\delta>Q^{*}(s,a_{1})+\delta\geq Q(s,a_{1}),s\in(\frac{\delta}{k},1],

Q(s,a_{1})\geq Q^{*}(s,a_{1})-\delta>Q^{*}(s,a_{2})+\delta\geq Q(s,a_{2}),s\in[-1,-\frac{\delta}{k}).

Then

m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q}_{sub}\right)\leq\frac{2\delta}{k}=O\left(\delta\right),

m\left(\mathcal{S}^{Q,\epsilon}_{adv}\right)\leq\frac{2\delta}{k}+2\epsilon=2\epsilon+O\left(\delta\right).

Therefore, the proof of the theorem is concluded.
∎

C.2
Stability of Bellman Optimality Equations

We propose the following concept of stability drawing on relevant research in the field of partial differential equations
(
Wang et al. 2022
)
.

Definition 47
.

Given two Banach spaces
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, if there exist
\delta>0
and
C>0
such that for all
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}
satisfying
\|\mathcal{T}Q-Q\|_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}<\delta
, we have that
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}<C\|\mathcal{T}Q-Q\|_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
, where
Q^{*}
is the exact solution of this functional equation. Then, we say that a nonlinear functional equation
\mathcal{T}Q=Q
is
\left(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}\right)
-stable.

Remark 48
.

This definition indicates that if
\mathcal{T}Q=Q
is
\left(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}\right)
-stable, then
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}=O\left(\|\mathcal{T}Q-Q\|_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\right)
, as
\|\mathcal{T}Q-Q\|_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\longrightarrow 0
,
\forall Q\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

Lemma 49
.

For any functions
f,g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we have

\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f(x)-g(x)\right).

Proof.

\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)=f(x_{f}^{*})-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)\leq f(x_{f}^{*})-g(x_{f}^{*})\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f(x)-g(x)\right),

where
x_{f}^{*}
is the maximizer of function
f
, i.e.
x_{f}^{*}=\arg\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)
.
∎

Lemma 50
.

For any functions
f,g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we have

\left|\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f+g\right)(x)-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)\right|\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|g(x)\right|.

Proof.

If
\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f+g\right)(x)\geq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f+g\right)(x)-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)+\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)

\displaystyle=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|g(x)\right|.

If
\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f+g\right)(x)<\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)
, we have

\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)-\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(f+g\right)(x)\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(-g(x)\right)\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|g(x)\right|,

where the first inequality comes from Lemma
49
.
∎

Theorem 51
.

For any MDP
\mathcal{M}
, let
C_{\mathbb{P},p}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}
. Assume
p
and
q
satisfy the following conditions:

C_{\mathbb{P},p}<\frac{1}{\gamma};\quad p\geq\max\left\{1,\frac{\log\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\right)+\log\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)}{\log\frac{1}{\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}}}\right\};\quad p\leq q\leq\infty.

Then, Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is
\left(L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable.

Proof.

For any
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
and
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\cap L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
, denote that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}Q(s,a)

\displaystyle:=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}Q

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q=r+\mathcal{L}_{0}Q-Q.

Let
Q^{*}
denote the Bellman optimality Q-function. Note that
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q^{*}=Q^{*}
and
\mathcal{L}Q^{*}=0
. Define

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=w_{Q}:=Q-Q^{*},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=f_{Q}:=\mathcal{L}Q=\mathcal{L}Q-\mathcal{L}Q^{*}.

Based on the above notations, we have

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}Q-\mathcal{L}Q^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{0}Q-Q-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}+Q^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{L}_{0}Q-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right)-\left(Q-Q^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=-w+\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\left|w(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|-f+\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\bigg|_{(s,a)}

\displaystyle\leq\left|f\right|+\left|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\bigg|_{(s,a)}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\left|f\right|+\left|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}+\left\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the last inequality comes from the Minkowski’s inequality. In the following, we analyze the relation between
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
and
\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.

\displaystyle\quad\left|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\bigg|_{(s,a)}

\displaystyle=\left|\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)+w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right)-\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left|\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)+w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right)-\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\left(\int_{s^{\prime}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}ds^{\prime}\right)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}.

where the second inequality comes from Lemma
50
and the last inequality comes from the Holder’s inequality. Let
C_{\mathbb{P},p}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left(\gamma\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\right)^{p}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}1d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle=\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the last inequality comes from
\left\|w\right\|_{l^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\left\|w\right\|_{l^{p}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}
. Thus, when
C_{\mathbb{P},p}<\frac{1}{\gamma}
and
p\geq\frac{\log\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\right)+\log\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)}{\log\frac{1}{\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}}}
and
q\geq p
, we have

\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\frac{\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}}{1-\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

(25)

where the last inequality comes from
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.
∎

Remark 52
.

Note that we have proved a stronger conclusion than stability because the equation (
25
) holds for all
Q
rather than for
Q
satisfying
\|\mathcal{T}Q-Q\|_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\longrightarrow 0
.

Remark 53
.

When
\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)
is a probability mass function, then we have that
C_{\mathbb{P},p}\leq 1<\frac{1}{\gamma}
holds for all
1<p\leq\infty
. Generally, note that
\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}C_{\mathbb{P},p}=1
and as a consequence, when
p
is large enough,
C_{\mathbb{P},p}<\frac{1}{\gamma}
holds.

C.3
Instability of Bellman Optimality Equations

Theorem 54
.

There exists a MDP
\mathcal{M}
such that Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is not
\left(L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable, for
1\leq p<\infty
.

Generally, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 55
.

There exists an MDP
\mathcal{M}
such that Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is not
\left(L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable, for all
1\leq q<p\leq\infty
.

Proof.

In order to show a Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is not
\left(L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable, it is sufficient and necessary to prove
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\forall\delta>0,\exists Q(s,a),\text{ such that }\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}<\delta,\text{ but }\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\geq n{\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}}.

Define an MDP
\mathcal{M}
where
\mathcal{S}=[-1,1],\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2}\},

\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{1})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s-0.1\right\}},\quad&&s\in[-0.9,1]\\
&\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s\right\}},&&else\end{aligned}\right.,\quad\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s+0.1\right\}},\quad&&s\in[-1,0.9]\\
&\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s\right\}},&&else\end{aligned}\right.,

r(s,a_{i})=k_{i}s,\ k_{2}\geq k_{1}>0
. The transition function is essentially a deterministic transition dynamic and for convenience, we denote that

p(s,a_{1})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &s-0.1,\quad&&s\in[-0.9,1]\\
&s,&&else\end{aligned}\right.,\quad p(s,a_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &s+0.1,\quad&&s\in[-1,0.9]\\
&s,&&else\end{aligned}\right..

Let
Q^{*}(s,a)=Q^{\pi^{*}}(s,a)
be the optimal Q-function, where
\pi^{*}
is the optimal policy.

We have
Q^{*}(s,a_{2})\geq Q^{*}(s,a_{1}),\ \forall s\geq 0
. To prove this, we define
\bar{\pi}(s)\equiv a_{2},\forall s\geq 0
, and thus

Q^{\bar{\pi}}(s,a_{2})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r(s_{i},a_{2}),

(26)

where
s_{0}=s\geq 0,\ s_{i}=p(s_{i-1},a_{2})\geq 0,\,i\geq 1.
Consider Q-function of any policy
\pi

Q^{\pi}(s,\alpha_{0})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r(\tilde{s}_{i},\pi(\tilde{s}_{i})),

(27)

where
\pi(\tilde{s}_{0})=\alpha_{0}\in\mathcal{A},\ \tilde{s}_{0}=s\geq 0,\ \tilde{s}_{i+1}=p(\tilde{s}_{i},\pi(\tilde{s}_{i}))
and
r(\tilde{s}_{i},\pi(\tilde{s}_{i}))=\pi(a_{1}|\tilde{s}_{i})r(\tilde{s}_{i},a_{1})+\pi(a_{2}|\tilde{s}_{i})r(\tilde{s}_{i},a_{2})
.

We first notice that all
s_{i}
and
\tilde{s}_{i}
lie on the grid points
\mathcal{S}\cap\{s+0.1z:\,z\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, actually,
-\lfloor\frac{1+s}{0.1}\rfloor\leq z\leq\lfloor\frac{1-s}{0.1}\rfloor
. In the following, we prove
s_{i}\geq\tilde{s}_{i},\ \forall i
. We consider the recursion method and suppose
s_{i}\geq\tilde{s}_{i}.
Then, we have the following two cases. If
s_{i}\leq 0.9
, we obtain

s_{i+1}=s_{i}+0.1\geq\tilde{s}_{i}+0.1\geq\tilde{s}_{i+1}.

If
s_{i}>0.9
, it follows from
z\leq\lfloor\frac{1-s}{0.1}\rfloor
that

s_{i+1}=s_{i}=s+0.1\lfloor\frac{1-s}{0.1}\rfloor\geq\tilde{s}_{i+1}.

Thus, we have
s_{i+1}\geq\tilde{s}_{i+1},\ \forall i
. Note that
s_{0}=\tilde{s}_{0}=s
, and by recursion, it can be obtained that
s_{i}\geq\tilde{s}_{i}
holds for all
i
.

Noticing that the reward
r(s,a)
is an increasing function in terms of
s
and satisfies
r(s,a_{2})\geq r(s,a_{1}),\forall s\geq 0,
we have

r(s_{i},a_{2})\geq r(s_{i},\alpha_{i})\geq r(\tilde{s}_{i},\alpha_{i}),\quad\forall\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{A},i=0,1,2,\dots,

where the second inequality is due to
s_{i}\geq\tilde{s}_{i}
. As a consequence,

r(s_{i},a_{2})\geq r(\tilde{s}_{i},\pi(\tilde{s}_{i})),\quad\forall i=0,1,2,\dots.

(28)

Combining (
26
), (
27
), and (
28
), we obtain that
Q^{\bar{\pi}}(s,a_{2})\geq Q^{\pi}(s,\alpha_{0})
. Further, with
\alpha_{0}=a_{2}
, we derive
\bar{\pi}(s)={\pi^{*}}(s)
on
s>0
. With
\alpha_{0}=a_{1}
, we derive
Q^{*}(s,a_{2})=Q^{\bar{\pi}}(s,a_{2})\geq Q^{*}(s,a_{1}),\ \forall s\geq 0.

We then prove that given
1\leq q<p
,
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\delta>0
, there exists
Q(s,a)
with
\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\delta
, such that
\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\geq n\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
. Let
Q(s,a_{1})=Q^{*}(s,a_{1}),

Q(s,a_{2})=Q^{*}(s,a_{2})+h\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(\frac{1}{4}\epsilon,\frac{3}{4}\epsilon)}+\frac{4h}{\epsilon}s\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\frac{1}{4}\epsilon]}+\left(-\frac{4h}{\epsilon}s+4h\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{[\frac{3}{4}\epsilon,\epsilon)},

where
h>0
,
\epsilon=\min\left\{\left(\frac{\delta}{3h}\right)^{q},\left(3n\cdot 2^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)^{-\frac{pq}{p-q}}\right\}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{A}(s)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&&s\in A\\
&0,&&else\end{aligned}\right.
denotes the indicator function. It can be seen from the definition that

Q^{*}(s,a_{2})\leq Q^{*}(s,a_{2})+h\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(\frac{1}{4}\epsilon,\frac{3}{4}\epsilon)}\leq Q(s,a_{2})\leq Q^{*}(s,a_{2})+h\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\epsilon)}.

(29)

We consider the following cases.

•

When
s\in(-0.1,-0.1+\epsilon),a=a_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{2})=r(s,a_{2})+\gamma\max_{a_{i}}Q(s+0.1,a_{i})=r(s,a_{2})+\gamma Q(s+0.1,a_{2}),

Together with (
29
), we have

\displaystyle Q(s,a_{2})=Q^{*}(s,a_{2})=r(s,a_{2})+\gamma Q^{*}(s+0.1,a_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{2})\leq r(s,a_{2})+\gamma[Q^{*}(s+0.1,a_{2})+h]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{*}(s,a_{2})+h\gamma=Q(s,a_{2})+h\gamma,

thus
|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{2})-Q(s,a_{2})|\leq h\gamma.

•

When
s\in(0,\epsilon),a=a_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{2})

\displaystyle=r(s,a_{2})+\gamma\max_{a_{i}}Q(s+0.1,a_{i})=r(s,a_{2})+\gamma Q(s+0.1,a_{2})

\displaystyle=r(s,a_{2})+\gamma Q^{*}(s+0.1,a_{2})=Q^{*}(s,a_{2}),

Again from (
29
), there is

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{2})-Q(s,a_{2})|=|Q^{*}(s,a_{2})-Q(s,a_{2})|\leq h.

•

When
s\in(0.1,0.1+\epsilon),a=a_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{1})=r(s,a_{1})+\gamma\max_{a_{i}}Q(s-0.1,a_{i})=r(s,a_{1})+\gamma Q(s-0.1,a_{2}),

Utilizing (
29
), we have

\displaystyle Q(s,a_{1})=Q^{*}(s,a_{1})=r(s,a_{1})+\gamma Q^{*}(s-0.1,a_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{1})\leq r(s,a_{1})+\gamma[Q^{*}(s-0.1,a_{2})+h]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{*}(s,a_{1})+h\gamma=Q(s,a_{1})+h\gamma,

thus
|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{1})-Q(s,a_{1})|\leq h\gamma.

•

Otherwise,

\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a_{i})=r(s,a_{i})+\gamma Q\left(p(s,a_{i}),\pi^{*}(p(s,a_{i}))\right)=Q^{*}(s,a_{i}),

also note that
Q(s,\cdot)=Q^{*}(s,\cdot)
for
s\notin(0,\epsilon)
, thus

|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)-Q(s,a)|=|Q^{*}(s,a)-Q(s,a)|=0.

From the analysis above, we have

\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq(2h\gamma+h)\epsilon^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq 3h\epsilon^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\delta,

(30)

and

\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\geq\|(Q-Q^{*})\mathbbm{1}_{(\frac{1}{4}\epsilon,\frac{3}{4}\epsilon)}\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\geq h\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq n\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

(31)

Inequality (
30
) and (
31
) come from
\epsilon=\min\left\{\left(\frac{\delta}{3h}\right)^{q},\left(3n\cdot 2^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)^{-\frac{pq}{p-q}}\right\}
, which prove the desired property.
∎

Appendix D
Theorems and Proofs of Stability Analysis of DQN

In practical DQN training, we use the following loss:

\mathcal{L}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\sum_{(s,a,r,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}}\left|r+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}Q(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\bar{\theta})-Q(s,a;\theta)\right|,

where
\mathcal{B}
represents a batch of transition pairs sampled from the replay buffer and
\bar{\theta}
is the parameter of target network.

\mathcal{L}(\theta)
is a approximation of the following objective:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(Q;\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(\cdot)}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi(\cdot|s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)-Q(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(\cdot,\cdot)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)-Q(s,a)\right|,

where
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim\mu_{0}}\left[(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\operatorname{Pr}^{\pi}(s_{t}=s|s_{0})\right]
is the state visitation distribution
and
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim\mu_{0}}\left[(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\operatorname{Pr}^{\pi}(s_{t}=s,a_{t}=a|s_{0})\right]
is the state-action visitation distribution.

D.1
Definition and Properties of Seminorm

Definition 56
.

Given a policy
\pi
, for any function
f:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we define the seminorm
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
.

•

If
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}
is a probability density function, we define

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle:=\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|^{p}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

•

If
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}
is a probability mass function, we define

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}:=\left(\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Remark 57
.

Note that
\mathcal{L}(Q;\pi)=\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{1,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.

Theorem 58
.

For any
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
is a seminorm.

Proof.

Firstly, we show that
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
satisfies the absolute homogeneity. For any function
f
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\lambda f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\left|\lambda\right|\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\left|\lambda\right|\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

Next, we show that the triangle inequality holds. For any functions
f
and
g
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f+g\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(f+g)\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}+\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}g\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}+\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the inequality comes from the triangle inequality of
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
.
∎

Theorem 59
.

If
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, then
