L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right):=\left\{f\left|\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\infty\right.\right\}
is a Banach space, for
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

Proof.

Firstly, we show the
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
is positive definite. If
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=0
, we have that
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)=0
, for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
. Due to the nonnegativity of
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)
, we have
f(s,a)=0
, for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
.

We show the completeness of
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
in the following. For any Cauchy sequence
\left\{f_{i}\right\}\subset L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
,
\left\{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}f_{i}\right\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
. Then, due to the completeness of
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
, there exists
g\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
such that

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}f_{i}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

Let
f=\frac{g}{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\left\|g\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\infty
and

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\left\|f_{i}\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

Thus,
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
is a Banach space.
∎

We analyze the properties of
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
in the following lemma.

Lemma 60
.

Given a policy
\pi
, for any function
f:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, then we have the following properties.

•

If
M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)<\infty
, then

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},\ \forall 1\leq p\leq\infty.

•

If
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\inf_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
, then we have

C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},\ \forall 1\leq p\leq\infty.

•

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},\ \forall 1\leq p\leq\infty
.

•

If
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\neq 0
for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, then we have

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},\ \forall 1<p<\infty,

where
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}=\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right|^{-\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}
.

•

If
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\neq 0
for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, then we have

\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},\ \forall 1<p<\infty,

where
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}=\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right|^{-\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}}
.

Proof.

(1)
If
M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|^{p}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|f(s,a)\right|^{p}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

(2)
If
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\inf_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|^{p}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\geq C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

(3)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|d\mu(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the first inequality comes from the Holder’s inequality.

(4)
For
1<p<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|^{p}d\mu(s,a)

\displaystyle\geq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}^{p}\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right|^{-\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{-(p-1)},

where the inequality comes from reverse Holder’s inequality.

(5)
Further, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{{p^{2}},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}^{p^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)f(s,a)\right|^{p^{2}}d\mu(s,a)

\displaystyle\geq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}^{p^{2}}\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right|^{-\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{-(p-1)},

where the inequality comes from reverse Holder’s inequality.
∎

Remark 61
.

Note that in a practical Q-learning scheme, we take the
\epsilon
-greedy policy for exploration and as a result, for any state-action pair
(s,a)
, we can visit it with positive probability, i.e.
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
. Furthermore, the condition,
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\neq 0
for almost everywhere
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, always holds.

D.2
Stability of DQN: the Good

Theorem 62
.

For any MDP
\mathcal{M}
and fixed policy
\pi
, assume
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\inf_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
and let
C_{\mathbb{P},p}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}
. Assume
p
and
q
satisfy the following conditions:

C_{\mathbb{P},p}<\frac{1}{\gamma};\quad p\geq\max\left\{1,\frac{\log\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\right)+\log\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)}{\log\frac{1}{\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}}}\right\};\quad p\leq q\leq\infty.

Then, Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is
\left(L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable.

Proof.

For any
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
and
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\cap L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
, denote that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}Q(s,a)

\displaystyle:=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}Q

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q=r+\mathcal{L}_{0}Q-Q.

Let
Q^{*}
denote the Bellman optimality Q-function. Note that
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q^{*}=Q^{*}
and
\mathcal{L}Q^{*}=0
. Define

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=w_{Q}:=Q-Q^{*},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=f_{Q}:=\mathcal{L}Q=\mathcal{L}Q-\mathcal{L}Q^{*}.

Based on the above notations, we have

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}Q-\mathcal{L}Q^{*}

(32)

\displaystyle=-w+\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}.

(33)

According to the inequality (
25
), we have that when
C_{\mathbb{P},p}<\frac{1}{\gamma}
and
p\geq\frac{\log\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\right)+\log\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)}{\log\frac{1}{\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}}}
and
q\geq p
, we have

\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\frac{\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}}{1-\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

According to Lemma
60
, when
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\inf_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
, we have

\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\frac{\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(1-\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

Therefore, the proof of the theorem is concluded.
∎

Remark 63
.

Note that in a practical Q-learning scheme, we take the
\epsilon
-greedy policy for exploration and as a result, for any state-action pair
(s,a)
, we can visit it with positive probability, and thus the condition
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}>0
is fulfilled.

We also demonstrate a theorem with better bound yet stronger conditions.

Theorem 64
.

For any MDP
\mathcal{M}
and fixed policy
\pi
, assume
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\inf_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
. Assume
p
,
q
and
\gamma
satisfy the following conditions:

C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},\mathbb{P},p}:=\frac{\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}}<\frac{1}{\gamma};\quad p\geq\frac{\log\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\right)+\log\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)}{\log\frac{1}{\gamma C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},\mathbb{P},p}}}-1;\quad q\geq p^{2}.

Then, Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is
\left(L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable.

Proof.

For any
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
and
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\cap L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
, denote that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}Q(s,a)

\displaystyle:=\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}Q

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q=r+\mathcal{L}_{0}Q-Q.

Let
Q^{*}
denote the Bellman optimality Q-function. Note that
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q^{*}=Q^{*}
and
\mathcal{L}Q^{*}=0
. Define

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=w_{Q}:=Q-Q^{*},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=f_{Q}:=\mathcal{L}Q=\mathcal{L}Q-\mathcal{L}Q^{*}.

Based on the above notations, we have

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}Q-\mathcal{L}Q^{*}

\displaystyle=-w+\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\left|w(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|-f+\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\bigg|_{(s,a)}

\displaystyle\leq\left|f\right|+\left|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\bigg|_{(s,a)}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\left|f\right|+d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\left|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}+\left\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the last inequality comes from the Minkowski’s inequality. Owing to Lemma
60
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\geq\frac{1}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}}\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}=\left(\int_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right|^{-\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}}
.
In the following, we analyze the relation between
\left\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
and
\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.

\displaystyle\quad\left|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right|\bigg|_{(s,a)}

\displaystyle=\left|\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)+w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right)-\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left|\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)+w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right)-\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\left(\int_{s^{\prime}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}ds^{\prime}\right)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)},

where the second inequality comes from Lemma
50
and the third inequality comes from the Holder’s inequality. let
C_{\mathbb{P},p}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}
and then, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(Q^{*}+w\right)-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left(\gamma\left\|\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right)^{p^{2}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left(d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right)^{p^{2}}d\mu(s,a)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{2}}}\gamma C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left\|\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|w\left(s,a\right)\right|\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the last inequality comes from
\left\|w\right\|_{l^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\left\|w\right\|_{l^{p}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}
.
Then, we have that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}}-\gamma\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}\right)\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}.

When
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\inf_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)>0
, we have

\displaystyle C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},p}\leq\frac{\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}}}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}}

Thus, when the following conditions hold

C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},\mathbb{P},p}:=\frac{\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}}<\frac{1}{\gamma};\quad p\geq\frac{\log\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\right)+\log\left(\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)}{\log\frac{1}{\gamma C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},\mathbb{P},p}}}-1;\quad q\geq p^{2},

we have

\displaystyle\left\|w\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}}}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}-\gamma\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}-\gamma\left\|d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}\right\|_{L^{p^{2}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}C_{\mathbb{P},p}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\right)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p^{2}}}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)},

where the last inequality comes from
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p^{2},d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\mu\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{2}}-\frac{1}{q}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.
∎

Remark 65
.

The conditions are not satisfactory. The result implicitly adds the constrain for
\gamma
because
\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi},\mathbb{P},p}=\frac{1}{C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}}
, which indicates
\gamma
may be very small, i.e.
\gamma<C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
.

In the following theorem, we describe the instablility of DQN.

Theorem 66
.

There exists a MDP
\mathcal{M}
such that for all
\pi
satisfying
M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)<\infty
, Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is not
\left(L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable, for all
1\leq q<p\leq\infty
.

Proof.

According to the proof of Theorem
55
, for
1\leq q<p\leq\infty
, there exists a MDP
\mathcal{M}
satisfying the following statement. For all
\delta>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
Q\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\cap L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
satisfying
\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\frac{\delta}{M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}}
such that
\left\|Q-Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}>n\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.

According to Lemma
60
, if
M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}:=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)<\infty
, we have

\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left\|Q\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}<\infty.

Thus, we have
Q\in L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\cap L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
. For the same reason, we have

\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\delta.

Hence, we get that For all
\delta>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
Q\in L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\cap L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
satisfying
\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\leq\delta
such that
\left\|Q-Q^{*}\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}>n\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\right\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}
.
Therefore, the proof of the theorem is concluded.
∎

Remark 67
.

If
M_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}=\infty
,
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}
degenerates to the discrete probability distribution. Then,
L^{p,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}
can be considered as a norm defined on a finite dimension space. In this setting, we also have
C_{d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}=0
and Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is not
\left(L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable, for any
p
and
q
.

According to the above theorems and remarks, we have the following corollary in the DQN procedure.

Corollary 68
.

In practical DQN procedure, the Bellman optimality equations
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is
(L^{\infty,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}),L^{p}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}))
-stable for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
, while it is not
(L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}),L^{p}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}))
-stable for all
1\leq q<p\leq\infty
.

D.3
Stability of DQN: the Bad

Theorem 69
.

There exists an MDP
\mathcal{M}
such that for all
\pi
satisfying
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}
is a discrete probability distribution, Bellman optimality equation
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q=Q
is not
\left(L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right),L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
-stable, for any
p
and
q
.

Proof.

We only need to show there exists an MDP such that
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\forall\delta>0,\exists Q(s,a),
such that
\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q-Q\|_{L^{q,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}<\delta,\text{ but }\|Q-Q^{*}\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}\geq n.

Consider an MDP
\mathcal{M}
where
\mathcal{S}=[-1,1],\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2}\},

\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{1})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s-0.1\right\}},\quad&&s\in[-0.9,1]\\
&\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s\right\}},&&else\end{aligned}\right.,\quad\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s+0.1\right\}},\quad&&s\in[-1,0.9]\\
&\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{s^{\prime}=s\right\}},&&else\end{aligned}\right.,

r(s,a_{i})=k_{i}s,\ k_{2}\geq k_{1}>0
. The transition function is essentially a deterministic transition dynamic and for convenience, we denote that

p(s,a_{1})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &s-0.1,\quad&&s\in[-0.9,1]\\
&s,&&else\end{aligned}\right.,\quad p(s,a_{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &s+0.1,\quad&&s\in[-1,0.9]\\
&s,&&else\end{aligned}\right..

Let
Q^{*}(s,a)=Q^{\pi^{*}}(s,a)
be the optimal Q-function, where
\pi^{*}
is the optimal policy.

Define
B_{0}=\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\exists a\in\mathcal{A},s.t.\,d^{\pi}_{\mu_{0}}(s,a)\neq 0\}
, which contains all the states that can be explored. Let
B=\left\{B_{0}\cup\{B_{0}+0.1\}\cup\{B_{0}-0.1\}\right\}\cap\mathcal{S}
, then
\forall s\in B,\ p(s,a)\in B.
Since
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}
is a discrete probability distribution,
\mu(B)=\mu(B_{0})=0.

Let
D=[-1,1]\setminus B
, and
Q(s,a)=Q^{*}(s,a)+h\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{D}
, where
h=\frac{n}{2^{\frac{1}{p}}}
. We have that
Q(s,a)=Q^{*}(s,a),\forall s\notin D.

We then have for any
s\in B
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\cdot\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q(p(s,a),a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s,a)+\gamma\cdot\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}(p(s,a),a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q^{*}(s,a)

\displaystyle=Q(s,a).

For any
s\notin B
,
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}(s,a)=0
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Hence,
\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)-Q(s,a)\|_{L^{q,d^{\pi}_{\mu_{0}}}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}=0<\delta.

However, we find that

\|Q(s,a)-Q^{*}(s,a)\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}=h\cdot\mu(D)^{\frac{1}{p}}=h\cdot 2^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq n,

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 70
.

If
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}
is a discrete probability distribution,
L^{\infty,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
is not a good choice. However, the sample process should be considered in practical reinforcement learning algorithms and as a consequence, we have to apply the space
L^{\infty,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
rather than
L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
.

Appendix E
Theorems and Proofs of Policy Robustness under k-measurement Error in Probability Space

E.1
Vulnerability of Non-infinity Measurement Errors

Lemma 71
.

(
Zhang 2023
)
Consider two probability measures
P
and
Q
that are absolutely continuous with respect to a reference measure
\mu
with density
p
and
q
, then

2\|P-Q\|^{2}_{TV}\leq\operatorname{KL}(P\|Q)\leq 2(3+\sup_{z}\ln\frac{p(z)}{q(z)})\|P-Q\|_{TV}

(34)

Theorem 72

(Vulnerability of Non-infinity Measurement Errors)
.

There exists an MDP such that the following statement holds. Let
\varphi
be a policy from the policy family
\mathcal{F}=\left\{\varphi\left|\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}\varphi(a|s)=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}\pi^{*}(a|s)\right.\right\}
. For any
\epsilon>0
,
1\leq k<\infty
,
\delta>0
and any state distribution
\mu
, there exists a policy
\pi
satisfying
\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)\leq\delta
or
\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\pi\|\varphi\right)\leq\delta
, such that
m\left(\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}\right)=O(\delta)
but
m\left(\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}\right)=m(\mathcal{S})
.

Proof.

Consider this given MDP: state space
\mathcal{S}=\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right]
, action space
\mathcal{A}=\left\{a_{1},a_{2}\right\}
, and the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
defined as follows:

\pi^{*}(a_{1}|s)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if}\ s\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
0,&\text{if}\ s\in\left[-\frac{1}{2},0\right)\end{cases}

\pi^{*}(a_{2}|s)=1-\pi^{*}(a_{1}|s).

Without loss of generality, let’s assume that
\mu(s)=1
. Let
\varphi
represent any policy such that
\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}\varphi(a|s)=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}\pi^{*}(a|s)
. Then we define
\tilde{\varphi}
:

\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)=\begin{cases}\varphi(a_{i}|s),&\text{if}\ t<\varphi(a_{i}|s)<1-t\\
t,&\text{if}\ \varphi(a_{i}|s)\leq t\\
1-t,&\text{if}\ \varphi(a_{i}|s)\geq 1-t\end{cases}

where
t
is a number that satisfies
0<t<\frac{1}{2}
.

Let’s define the following set:

D_{\epsilon}=\cap_{i=0}^{n}(B_{i}\cap B_{i}^{\prime}),

where
B_{i}=\left[2i\epsilon,2i\epsilon+l\right]
,
B_{i}^{\prime}=\left[-2i\epsilon-l,-2i\epsilon\right]
,
n=\left[\frac{1-2l}{4\epsilon}\right]
, and
l=\min\{\frac{\delta^{k}}{2(n+1)^{2}(12+4\ln\frac{1-t}{t})^{k}},\epsilon\}
.

We introduce the policy
\pi
as shown: for
s
in
\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
,
\pi(a_{1}|s)
is formulated as:

\pi(a_{1}|s)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|s),&\text{if}\ s\notin D_{\epsilon}\\
\frac{t-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|2i\epsilon)}{l/2}(s-2i\epsilon)+\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|2i\epsilon),&\text{if}\ s\in\left[2i\epsilon,2i\epsilon+\frac{l}{2}\right]\\
-\frac{t-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|2i\epsilon+l)}{l/2}(s-2i\epsilon-\frac{l}{2})+t,&\text{if}\ s\in\left[2i\epsilon+\frac{l}{2},2i\epsilon+l\right]\end{cases}

for
s
in
\left[-\frac{1}{2},0\right)
,
\pi(a_{2}|s)
is formulated as:

\pi(a_{2}|s)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varphi}(a_{2}|s),&\text{if}\ s\notin D_{\epsilon}\\
\frac{t-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{2}|-2i\epsilon-l)}{l/2}(s+2i\epsilon+l)+\tilde{\varphi}(a_{2}|-2i\epsilon-l),&\text{if}\ s\in\left[-2i\epsilon-l,-2i\epsilon-\frac{l}{2}\right]\\
-\frac{t-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{2}|-2i\epsilon-\frac{l}{2})}{l/2}(s+2i\epsilon+\frac{l}{2})+t,&\text{if}\ s\in\left[-2i\epsilon-\frac{l}{2},-2i\epsilon\right]\end{cases}

where
i\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
.

Then we can calculate
\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)=\left\|\mu\operatorname{KL}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)\right\|_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\tilde{\varphi}\right)+\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\|\pi\right)+\left(\int_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\varphi(a_{i}|s)-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)\right)\ln\frac{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)}{\pi(a_{i}|s)}\right|^{k}d\mu(s)\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}.

Similarly, we get the same conclusion when
s<0
.

When
s\geq 0
and
\varphi\neq\tilde{\varphi}
, we have
\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|s)=1-t
,
\tilde{\varphi}(a_{2}|s)=t
,
1-t\leq\varphi(a_{1}|s)\leq 1
,
0\leq\varphi(a_{2}|s)\leq t
. Therefore, through calculation, we know that:

\left|\varphi(a_{1}|s)\ln\frac{\varphi(a_{1}|s)}{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|s)}\right|\leq\ln\frac{1}{1-t},\ \left|\varphi(a_{2}|s)\ln\frac{\varphi(a_{2}|s)}{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{2}|s)}\right|\leq\frac{t}{e}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\tilde{\varphi}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\varphi(a_{i}|s)\ln\frac{\varphi(a_{i}|s)}{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)}\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\tilde{\varphi}\neq\varphi}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\varphi(a_{i}|s)\ln\frac{\varphi(a_{i}|s)}{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)}\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\tilde{\varphi}\neq\varphi}\left|\ln\frac{1}{1-t}+\frac{t}{e}\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\ln\frac{1}{1-t}+\frac{t}{e}.

From the definition of
\pi
and
\tilde{\varphi}
, we know that
t\leq\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)\leq 1-t
,
t\leq\pi(a_{i}|s)\leq 1-t
,
|\varphi(a_{i}|s)-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)|\leq t
where
i=1,2.
So we have :

\displaystyle\left(\int_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\varphi(a_{i}|s)-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)\right)\ln\frac{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)}{\pi(a_{i}|s)}\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|2t\ln\frac{1-t}{t}\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq 2t\ln\frac{1-t}{t}.

We can easily verify that

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\left(\ln\frac{1}{1-t}+\frac{t}{e}+2t\ln\frac{1-t}{t}\right)=0

(35)

So we can fix a
t
s.t.
\ln\frac{1}{1-t}+\frac{t}{e}+2t\ln\frac{1-t}{t}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}
, i.e.

\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\tilde{\varphi}\right)+\left(\int_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\varphi(a_{i}|s)-\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)\right)\ln\frac{\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)}{\pi(a_{i}|s)}\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}

(36)

Start calculating
\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\|\pi\right)
from the right side of inequality (
34
) in lemma
71
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\|\pi\right)=\left\|\mu\operatorname{KL}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\|\pi\right)\right\|_{k}

(37)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left|\mu(s)\operatorname{KL}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\cdot|s)\|\pi(\cdot|s)\right)\right|^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{s\in D_{\epsilon}}\left((3+\sup_{a}|\ln\frac{\tilde{\varphi}(a|s)}{\pi(a|s)})|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)-\pi(a_{i}|s)\right|\right)^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{s\in D_{\epsilon}}\left(2(3+\ln\frac{1-t}{t})\left|\tilde{\varphi}(a_{1}|s)-\pi(a_{1}|s)\right|\right)^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left((2(3+\ln\frac{1-t}{t})^{k}\int_{s\in D_{\epsilon}}\left(\left|\tilde{\varphi}(a_{i}|s)-\pi(a_{i}|s)\right|\right)^{k}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left((2(3+\ln\frac{1-t}{t})^{k}2(n+1)l\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta}{2}.

By combining formula (
36
) with formula (
37
), it can be inferred that

\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)\leq\delta.

Let’s now consider the reverse KL. In this situation, We have to assume that
\varphi
is stochastic because of the restriction that the denominator cannot be zero. Then, there must be a number
t
such that
0<t<\min_{a\in\mathcal{A},s\in\mathcal{S}}\varphi(a|s)
, and we fix this
t
. Thus,
\varphi=\tilde{\varphi}
. We only need to consider
\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\pi\|\tilde{\varphi}\right)
.

Obviously, the equation (
35
) is symmetric with respect to
\pi
and
\varphi
, so we also have :

\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\pi\|\varphi\right)=\mathcal{D}_{k,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\pi\|\tilde{\varphi}\right)\leq\delta.

If
s\in\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}
, then there must hold:
\pi(a_{1}|s)\leq\pi(a_{2}|s)
when
s\geq 0
and
\pi(a_{1}|s)>\pi(a_{2}|s)
when
s<0
. According to the definition of
\pi
, in order to meet the above conditions, there must exist
s\in D_{\epsilon}
. Therefore:

\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}\subseteq D_{\epsilon}.

Hence,

m\left(\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}\right)\leq m\left(D_{\epsilon}\right)=2(n+1)l\leq\frac{\delta^{k}}{(n+1)M^{k}}=O(\delta^{k}).

Let
s_{i}=2i\epsilon+\frac{l}{2},\ i=0,1,\dots,n
. From the defination of
\pi
, we know that
\pi(a_{1}|s_{i})=t<\pi(a_{2}|s_{i})
. This is used to represent the points on the positive real axis where
\pi(a_{1}|\cdot)
takes on the value of
t
. Combined with the continuity of the function
\pi
at
s_{i}
, there must exist a neighborhood
B_{\epsilon_{i}}(s_{i})
, s.t.
\pi(a_{1}|s)<\pi(a_{2}|s)
for
\forall\ s\in B_{\epsilon_{i}}(s_{i})
, where
i=0,1,\dots,n
.

We now show
\left[s_{i},s_{i+1}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}
, where
i=0,1,\dots,n-1.
For
\forall\ s\in\left[s_{i},s_{i+1}\right]
, it is obvious that

B_{\epsilon}(s)\cap\left(\left[s_{i},s_{i}+\epsilon_{i}\right)\cup\left(s_{i+1}-\epsilon_{i+1},s_{i+1}\right]\right)\neq\emptyset,

because
s_{i+1}-s_{i}=2\epsilon
, where
i=0,1,\dots,n-1
.

Therefore,
\exists\ s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)\cap\left(\left[s_{i},s_{i}+\epsilon_{i}\right)\cup\left(s_{i+1}-\epsilon_{i+1},s_{i+1}\right]\right)\subseteq B_{\epsilon}
, s.t.

Q^{*}(s,\arg\max_{a}\pi(a|s_{\nu}))=Q^{*}(s,a_{2})<\max_{a}Q(s,a).

So we have

\left[s_{i},s_{i+1}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon},

where
i=0,1,\dots,n-1
.

As
\pi(a_{1}|\frac{l}{2})=t_{2}\leq\pi(a_{2}|\frac{l}{2})
, it follows that
\arg\max_{a}\pi(a|\frac{l}{2})=a_{2}
, thus
\left[0,s_{0}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}
, and similarly
\left[s_{n},\frac{1}{2}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}
.

In conclusion, we can get
\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}
. The proof of
\left[-\frac{1}{2},0\right)\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}
follows a similar process and does not need further elaboration.

Therefore,

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}.

So we have :

m\left(\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}\right)=m(\mathcal{S}).

Therefore, the proof of the theorem is concluded.
∎

E.2
Robustness Guarantee under Infinity Measurement Error

Theorem 73

(Robustness Guarantee under Infinity Measurement Error)
.

For any MDP and any state distribution
\mu
satisfying
\mu(s)>0
for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, let
S_{\delta}=\{s\mid\exists\ a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},\ \text{s.t.}\ |\varphi(a|s)-\varphi(a^{\prime}|s)|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{2\delta}{\mu(s)}}\}
and
h(\delta)=\mu(S_{\delta})
. Then,
h(\delta)
is a monotonic function with
h(0)=0
. Let
\varphi
be a policy from the policy family
\mathcal{F}=\left\{\varphi\mid\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}\varphi(a|s)=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}\pi^{*}(a|s)\right\}
. If
S_{\delta}
is the union of finite connected subsets, then for any
\delta>0
and any policy
\pi
satisfying
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)\leq\delta
or
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\pi\|\varphi\right)\leq\delta
, we have that
m\left(\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}\right)=O(h(\delta))
and
m\left(\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}\right)=2\epsilon+O(h(\delta))
.

Proof.

From
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\varphi\|\pi\right)=\|\mu(s)\operatorname{KL}(\varphi(\cdot|s)\|\pi(\cdot|s))\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})\times\operatorname{KL}(\mathcal{A})}\leq\delta
, we can deduce that

\mu(s)\operatorname{KL}(\varphi(\cdot|s)\|\pi(\cdot|s))\leq\delta,\quad\forall\ s\in\mathcal{S}.

Then according to inequality (
34
) , we can derive that

\displaystyle\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}|\varphi(a|s)-\pi(a|s)|\leq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}|\varphi(a|s)-\pi(a|s)|

(38)

\displaystyle=\|\varphi(\cdot|s)-\pi(\cdot|s)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{A})}=2\|\varphi(\cdot|s)-\pi(\cdot|s))\|_{{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\operatorname{KL}(\varphi(\cdot|s)\|\pi(\cdot|s))}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\delta}{\mu(s)}}.

Then, as long as
|\varphi(a|s)-\varphi(a^{\prime}|s)|>2\sqrt{\frac{2\delta}{\mu(s)}}
, for
\forall\ a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, we can deduce
s\notin\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}
. Hence, we have that

\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi}\subseteq S_{\delta},\ \mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon}\subseteq S_{\delta}+B_{\epsilon}.

Therefore,

m(\mathcal{S}_{sub}^{\pi})\leq m(S_{\delta})=h(\delta),

and

\displaystyle m(\mathcal{S}_{adv}^{\pi,\epsilon})\leq m(S_{\delta}+B_{\epsilon})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(S_{\delta_{i}}+B_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq m(S_{\delta})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(m(S_{\delta_{i}}+B_{\epsilon})-m(B_{\epsilon}))

\displaystyle=h(\delta)+O(\epsilon).

From the derivation of equation (
38
), we can see that the whole process is symmetrical with respect to
\pi
and
\varphi
, so this conclusion also applies to reverse
\operatorname{KL}
, i.e. when policy
\pi
satisfying
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu}\left(\pi\|\varphi\right)\leq\delta
.
∎

Appendix F
Derivation of CAR-RL

In this section, we show the derivation of CAR-DQN and CAR-PPO loss functions.

F.1
Derivation of CAR-DQN

Our theory motivates us to use the following objective:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\left\|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}-Q_{\theta}\right\|_{L^{\infty,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\left|r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\theta}\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]-Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|.

However, the objective is intractable in a model-free setting, due to the unknown environment, i.e. unknown reward function and unknown transition function.

Remark 74
.

We apply the space
L^{\infty,d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
rather than
L^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\right)
because the sampling process should be considered in practical reinforcement learning algorithms.

F.1.1
Surrogate Objective

We can derive that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|.

However, in a practical reinforcement learning setting, we cannot directly get the estimation of
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)
.

Theorem 75
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{train}(\theta):=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|
and
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta):=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|
. We have that

\left|\mathcal{L}_{car}^{train}(\theta)-\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta)\right|\leq\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)\leq\mathcal{L}_{car}^{train}(\theta)+\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta).

Proof.

On one hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left(\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|+\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|-\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|\right|,

where the second inequality comes from Lemma
50
.
∎

It is hard to calculate or estimate
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta)
in practice. Fortunately, we think
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta)
should be small in practice and we give a constant upper bound of
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta)
in the smooth environment.

Lemma 76
.

Suppose
Q
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r}\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(\cdot,a)
is uniformly bounded, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)\right|\leq C_{Q},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A},

where
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
. Further, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}Q(\cdot,a)
has the same uniform bound as
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(\cdot,a)
, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}Q(s,a)\right|\leq C_{Q},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|r(s,a)\right|+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left|\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq M_{r}+\gamma M_{Q}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\},\qquad\forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}.

Let
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
. Suppose the inequality (
76
) holds for
k=n
. Then, for
k=n+1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}^{n+1}Q(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|r(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{T}_{B}^{n}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|r(s,a)\right|+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,a)}\left|\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{T}_{B}^{n}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq M_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma)C_{Q}+\gamma C_{Q}

\displaystyle=C_{Q}.

By induction, we have
\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}Q(s,a)\right|\leq C_{Q},\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A},k\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Lemma 77
.

Suppose the environment is
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth and suppose
Q
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r}\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(\cdot,a)
is Lipschitz continuous, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s,a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s^{\prime},a)\right|\leq L_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}\|s-s^{\prime}\|,

where
L_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}=L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}
and
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
. Further, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}Q(\cdot,a)
is Lipschitz continuous and has the same Lipschitz constant as
\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(\cdot,a)
, i.e.

\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}Q(s,a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}Q(s^{\prime},a)\right|\leq L_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}\|s-s^{\prime}\|.

Proof.

For all
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s_{1},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s_{2},a)

\displaystyle=r(s_{1},a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s_{1},a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]-r(s_{2},a)-\gamma\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s_{2},a)}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(r(s_{1},a)-r(s_{2},a)\right)+\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right)\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)ds^{\prime}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\quad\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s_{1},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q(s_{2},a)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left(r(s_{1},a)-r(s_{2},a)\right)\right|+\left|\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right)\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)ds^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\leq L_{r}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|+\gamma\int_{s^{\prime}}\left|\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right|\left|\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\right)\right|ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq L_{r}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|+\gamma C_{Q}\int_{s^{\prime}}\left|\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{1},a)-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s_{2},a)\right|ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq L_{r}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|

\displaystyle=\left(L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)\|s_{1}-s_{2}\|.

The second inequality comes from the Lipschitz property of
r
. The third inequality comes from the uniform boundedness of
Q
and the last inequality utilizes the Lipschitz property of
\mathbb{P}
.

Note that the uniform boundedness used in the above proof is
C_{Q}
rather than
M_{Q}
. Then, due to lemma
76
, we can extend the above proof to
\mathcal{T}_{B}^{k}
.
∎

Theorem 78
.

Suppose the environment is
\left(L_{r},L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)
-smooth and suppose
Q_{\theta}
and
r
are uniformly bounded, i.e.
\exists\ M_{Q},M_{r}>0
such that
\left|Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|\leq M_{Q},\ \left|r(s,a)\right|\leq M_{r}\ \forall s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. If
M:=\sup_{\theta,(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)<\infty
, then we have

\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta)\leq C_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}\epsilon,

where
C_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}=L_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}M
,
L_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}=L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}
and
C_{Q}=\max\left\{M_{Q},\frac{M_{r}}{1-\gamma}\right\}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}^{diff}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)-\mathcal{T}_{B}Q_{\theta}(s,a)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left(L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)\|s_{\nu}-s\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)\epsilon\sup_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq M\left(L_{r}+\gamma C_{Q}L_{\mathbb{P}}\right)\epsilon,

where the first inequality comes from Lemma
77
and the last inequality comes from the uniform boundedness of
d_{\mu_{0}}^{\pi_{\theta}}
.
∎

F.1.2
Approximate Objective

Lemma 79
.

For any function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda>0
, we have

\max_{p\in\Delta(\Omega)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p}f(\omega)-\lambda\operatorname{KL}(p\|p_{0})\right]=\lambda\ln{\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p_{0}}\left[e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}\right]},

where
\Delta(\Omega)
denotes the set of probability distributions on
\Omega
. And the solution is achieved by the following distribution
q
:

q(\omega)=\frac{p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}}{\int_{\omega\in\Omega}p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}d\mu(\omega)}=\frac{1}{C}p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}.

Proof.

Let

C:=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p_{0}}\left[e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}\right]=\int_{\omega\in\Omega}p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}d\mu(\omega),

q(\omega)=\frac{p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}}{\int_{\omega\in\Omega}p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}d\mu(\omega)}=\frac{1}{C}p_{0}(\omega)e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p}f(\omega)-\lambda\operatorname{KL}(p\|p_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p}\left[\lambda\ln{e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}}-\lambda\ln{\frac{p(\omega)}{p_{0}(\omega)}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p}\left[\ln{\frac{e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}p_{0}(\omega)}{p(\omega)}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p}\left[\ln{\frac{Cq(\omega)}{p(\omega)}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left[\ln{C}-\operatorname{KL}(p\|q)\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda\ln{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\ln{\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p_{0}}\left[e^{\frac{f(\omega)}{\lambda}}\right]}.

Note that the equal sign holds if and only if
p=q
. Thus, we get

q\in\arg\max_{p\in\Delta(\Omega)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim p}f(\omega)-\lambda\operatorname{KL}(p\|p_{0})\right].

Therefore, the proof of the lemma is concluded.
∎

We get the following approximate objective of
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{train}(\theta)
:

\mathcal{L}_{car}^{app}(\theta)=\max_{(s,a,r,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}}\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s)}\left|r+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}Q_{\bar{\theta}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a)\right|.

Denote

f_{i}=f(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime}):=\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s_{i})}\left|r_{i}+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}Q_{\bar{\theta}}(s_{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a_{i})\right|.

To fully utilize each sample in the batch, we derive the soft version of the above objective:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}^{app}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\max_{(s,a,r,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}}\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}f(s,a,r,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\max_{p\in\Delta\left(\mathcal{B}\right)}\sum_{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}}p_{i}f(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})

(39)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\max_{p\in\Delta\left(\mathcal{B}\right)}\left(\sum_{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}}p_{i}f(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})-\lambda\operatorname{KL}\left(p\|U\left(\mathcal{B}\right)\right)\right),

(40)

where
U\left(\mathcal{B}\right)
represents the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{B}
. According to Lemma
79
, the optimal solution of the maximization problem (
40
) is
p^{*}
:

p_{i}^{*}=\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}.

The maximization problem (
40
) is the lower bound of the maximization problem (
39
) so
p^{*}
is a proper approximation of the optimal solution of the maximization problem (
39
). Thus, we get the soft version of the CAR objective:

\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\sum_{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}\max_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon}(s_{i})}\left|r_{i}+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}Q_{\bar{\theta}}(s_{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})-Q_{\theta}(s_{\nu},a_{i})\right|.

F.2
Derivation of CAR-PPO

Our theoretical results motivate us to optimize the following objective:

\displaystyle\quad\ \min_{\theta}\quad\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\min_{\theta}\quad\mathcal{D}_{\infty,\operatorname{KL}}^{\mu_{t}}\left(\operatorname{clip}(\pi_{\theta})\|\varphi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\min_{\theta}\quad\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\ \mu_{t}(s)\operatorname{KL}\left(\operatorname{clip}(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s))\|\varphi_{t}(\cdot\mid s)\right)

\displaystyle=\min_{\theta}\quad\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\ \mu_{t}(s)\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s)\right)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{clip}(\pi_{\theta}(a\mid s))A^{\pi_{t}}(a\mid s)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\theta}\quad\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\ \mu_{t}(s)\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s)\right)-\min_{s_{\nu}\in B(s)}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{clip}(\pi_{\theta}(a\mid s_{\nu}))A^{\pi_{t}}(a\mid s)\right)

\displaystyle=\min_{\theta}\quad\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\ \mu_{t}(s)\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s)\right)-\min_{s_{\nu}\in B(s)}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi_{t}(\cdot\mid s)}\operatorname{clip}\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a\mid s_{\nu})}{\pi_{t}(a\mid s)}\right)A^{\pi_{t}}(a\mid s)\right)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\theta}\quad\mathcal{L}_{car}^{train}(\theta),

where
\mathcal{H}(\cdot)
represents the entropy.

Furthermore, we get the following approximate objective of
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{train}(\theta)
considering the practice sampling process:

\mathcal{L}_{car}^{app}(\theta)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\max_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{B}}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|s)\right)-\min_{s_{\nu}\in B(s)}\operatorname{clip}\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a|s_{\nu})}{\pi_{t}(a|s)}\right)A^{\pi_{t}}(a|s)\right).

Denote

f_{i}=f(s_{i},a_{i}):=-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|s_{i})\right)-\min_{s_{\nu}\in B(s_{i})}\operatorname{clip}\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{i}|s_{\nu})}{\pi_{t}(a_{i}|s_{i})}\right)A^{\pi_{t}}(a_{i}|s_{i}).

To effectively use each sample in a batch, we introduce a soft version of the CAR objective:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{car}^{app}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\max_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{B}}f(s_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\max_{p\in\Delta\left(\mathcal{B}\right)}\sum_{(s_{i},a_{i})\in\mathcal{B}}p_{i}f(s_{i},a_{i})

(41)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\max_{p\in\Delta\left(\mathcal{B}\right)}\left(\sum_{(s_{i},a_{i})\in\mathcal{B}}p_{i}f(s_{i},a_{i})-\lambda\operatorname{KL}\left(p\|U\left(\mathcal{B}\right)\right)\right),

(42)

where
U\left(\mathcal{B}\right)
represents the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{B}
. According to Lemma
79
, the optimal solution of the maximization problem (
42
) is
p^{*}
:

p_{i}^{*}=\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}.

The maximization problem (
42
) is the lower bound of the maximization problem (
41
) so
p^{*}
is a proper approximation of the optimal solution of the maximization problem (
41
). Thus, we get the soft version of the CAR objective:

\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\sum_{(s_{i},a_{i})\in\mathcal{B}}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{i}}}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|s_{i})\right)-\min_{s_{\nu}\in B(s_{i})}\operatorname{clip}\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{i}|s_{\nu})}{\pi_{t}(a_{i}|s_{i})}\right)A^{\pi_{t}}(a_{i}|s_{i})\right).

Appendix G
Additional Algorithm Details

G.1
Overall CAR-DQN and CAR-PPO Algorithm

We present the CAR-DQN training algorithm in Algorithm
1
and the CAR-PPO in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 1

Consistent Adversarial Robust Deep Q-network (CAR-DQN).

0:

Number of iterations
T
, target network update frequency
M
, a schedule
\beta_{t}
for the exploration probability
\beta
, a schedule
\epsilon_{t}
for the perturbation radius
\epsilon
, soft coefficient
\lambda
for soft CAR objective.

1:

Initialize current Q network
Q(s,a)
with parameters
\theta
and target Q network
Q^{\prime}(s,a)
with parameters
\theta^{\prime}\leftarrow\theta
.

2:

Initialize replay buffer
\mathcal{B}
.

3:

for

t=1

to

T

do

4:

With probability
\beta_{t}
select random action
a_{t}
, otherwise select the greedy action
a_{t}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a}Q(s_{t},a;\theta)
.

5:

Execute action
a_{t}
in environment and observe reward
r_{t}
and the next state
s_{t+1}
.

6:

Store transition pair
\left\{s_{t},a_{t},r_{t},s_{t+1}\right\}
in
\mathcal{B}
.

7:

Randomly sample a minibatch of
N
transition pairs
\left\{s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i+1}\right\}
from
\mathcal{B}
.

8:

Set
y_{i}=r_{i}+\gamma Q^{\prime}(s_{i+1},\mathop{\arg\max}_{a^{\prime}}Q(s_{i},a^{\prime};\theta);\theta^{\prime})
for non-terminal
s_{i}
, and
y_{i}=r_{i}
for terminal
s_{i}
.

9:

Option 1: Use projected gradient descent (PGD) to solve
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)
.

10:

For every
i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}
, run PGD to solve:

s_{i,\nu}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon_{t}}(s_{i})}\left|y_{i}-Q(s_{\nu},a_{i};\theta)\right|.

11:

Compute the approximation of
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)
:

\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}\alpha_{i}\left|y_{i}-Q(s_{i,\nu},a_{i};\theta)\right|,

where
\alpha_{i}=\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}\left|y_{i}-Q(s_{i,\nu},a_{i};\theta)\right|}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}\left|y_{i}-Q(s_{i,\nu},a_{i};\theta)\right|}}
.

12:

Option 2: Use convex relaxations of neural networks to solve a surrogate loss for
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)
.

13:

For every
i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}
, obtain upper and lower bounds on
Q(s,a_{i};\theta)
for all
s\in B_{\epsilon_{t}}(s_{i})
:

u_{i}(\theta)=\operatorname{ConvexRelaxUB}\left(Q(s,a_{i};\theta),\theta,s\in B_{\epsilon_{t}}(s_{i})\right),

l_{i}(\theta)=\operatorname{ConvexRelaxLB}\left(Q(s,a_{i};\theta),\theta,s\in B_{\epsilon_{t}}(s_{i})\right).

14:

Compute the surrogate loss for
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)
:

\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}\alpha_{i}\max\left\{\left|y_{i}-u_{i}(\theta)\right|,\left|y_{i}-l_{i}(\theta)\right|\right\},

where
\alpha_{i}=\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}\max\left\{\left|y_{i}-u_{i}(\theta)\right|,\left|y_{i}-l_{i}(\theta)\right|\right\}}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\left|B\right|}}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}\max\left\{\left|y_{i}-u_{i}(\theta)\right|,\left|y_{i}-l_{i}(\theta)\right|\right\}}}
.

15:

Update the Q network by performing a gradient descent step to minimize
\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)
.

16:

Update target Q network every
M
steps:
\theta^{\prime}\leftarrow\theta
.

17:

end

for

Algorithm 2

Consistent Adversarial Robust Proximal Policy Optimization (CAR-PPO).

0:

Number of iterations
T
, a schedule
\epsilon_{t}
for the perturbation radius
\epsilon
, robustness weighting
\kappa
, soft coefficient
\lambda
for soft CAR objective.

1:

Initialize policy network
\pi_{\theta}(a|s)
and value network
V_{\theta_{V}}(s)
.

2:

for

t=1

to

T

do

3:

Run
\pi_{\theta_{t}}
in the environment to collect a set of trajectories
\mathcal{D}=\{\tau_{k}\}
containing
|\mathcal{D}|
episodes, each
\tau_{k}
is a trajectory containing
|\tau_{k}|
samples,
\tau_{k}:=\{(s_{k,i},a_{k,i},r_{k,i},s_{k,i+1})\},i\in[|\tau_{k}|]
.

4:

Compute reward-to-go
\hat{R}_{k,i}
for each step
i
in every episode
k
using the trajectories and discount factor
\gamma
.

5:

Update value network
V_{\theta_{V}}(s)
by regression on the mean-square error:

\theta_{V}\leftarrow\underset{\theta_{V}}{\arg\min}\ \frac{1}{\sum_{k}\left|\tau_{k}\right|}\sum_{\tau_{k}\in D}\sum_{i=0}^{\left|\tau_{k}\right|}\left(V\left(s_{k,i}\right)-\hat{R}_{k,i}\right)^{2}.

6:

Estimate advantage
\hat{A}_{k,i}
for each step
i
in every episode
k
using generalized advantage estimation (GAE) and the current value function
V_{\theta_{V}}(s)
.

7:

Solve
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)
using projected gradient descent (PGD) or stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD):

8:

For all
k,i
, run PGD or SGLD to solve (the objective can be solved in a batch):

s_{i,\nu}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{s_{\nu}\in B_{\epsilon_{t}}(s_{k,i})}g\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{k,i}|s_{\nu})}{\pi_{\theta_{t}}(a_{k,i}|s_{k,i})},\hat{A}_{k,i}\right).

9:

Compute the approximation of
\mathcal{L}_{car}^{soft}(\theta)
:

\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sum_{k}\left|\tau_{k}\right|}\sum_{\tau_{k}\in D}\sum_{i=0}^{\left|\tau_{k}\right|}\alpha_{i}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|s_{k,i})\right)-g\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{k,i}|s_{i,\nu})}{\pi_{\theta_{t}}(a_{k,i}|s_{k,i})},\hat{A}_{k,i}\right)\right),

where

\alpha_{i}=\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|s_{k,i})\right)-g\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{k,i}|s_{i,\nu})}{\pi_{\theta_{t}}(a_{k,i}|s_{k,i})},\hat{A}_{k,i}\right)\right)}}{\sum_{\tau_{k}\in D}\sum_{i=0}^{\left|\tau_{k}\right|}e^{\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|s_{k,i})\right)-g\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{k,i}|s_{i,\nu})}{\pi_{\theta_{t}}(a_{k,i}|s_{k,i})},\hat{A}_{k,i}\right)\right)}}.

10:

Update the policy network by minimizing the vanilla PPO objective and the CAR objective (the minimization is solved using ADAM):

\theta_{t+1}\leftarrow\underset{\theta}{\arg\min}\ \frac{1}{\sum_{k}\left|\tau_{k}\right|}\sum_{\tau_{k}\in D}\sum_{i=0}^{\left|\tau_{k}\right|}g\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{k,i}|s_{k,i})}{\pi_{\theta_{t}}(a_{k,i}|s_{k,i})},\hat{A}_{k,i}\right)+\kappa\cdot\mathcal{L}_{car}(\theta).

11:

end

for

G.2
Additional Implementation Details

Pre-process in Atari Games.

We pre-process the input images into
84\times 84
grayscale images and normalize pixel values to the range
[0,1]
. In each environment, agents execute an action every 4 frames, skipping the other frames without frame stacking, and all rewards are clipped to the range
[-1,1]
.

DQN Architecture.

We implement Dueling network architectures
Wang et al. 2016
and the same architecture following
Zhang et al. 2020b
;
Oikarinen et al. 2021
which has 3 convolutional layers and a two-head fully connected layers. The first convolutional layer has
8\times 8
kernel, stride 4, and 32 channels. The second convolutional layer has
4\times 4
kernel, stride 2, and 64 channels. The third convolutional layer has
3\times 3
kernel, stride 1, and 64 channels and is then flattened. The fully connected layers have 512 units for both heads wherein one head outputs a state value and the other outputs advantages of each action. The ReLU activation function applies to every middle layer.

PPO Details.

We implement all PPO agents with the same fully connected (MLP) structure as
Zhang et al. 2020b
;
Oikarinen et al. 2021
. In the Ant environment, we choose the best regularization parameter
\kappa
in
\{0.01,0.03,0.1,0.3,1.0\}
for SA-PPO, RADIAL-PPO, and WocaR-PPO to achieve better robustness. For fair and comparable agent selection, we conduct multiple experiments for each setup, repeating each 17 times to account for the inherent performance variability in RL. After training, we attack all agents using random, critic, MAD and RS attacks. Then, we select the median agent by considering natural and these robust returns as our final agent. This chosen agent is then attacked using the SA-RL and PA-AD attacks to further robustness evaluation, because these attacks involve quite high computational costs.

Appendix H
Additional Experiment Results

H.1
Additional Comparative Results

Additional Comparisons.
Comparative results for baselines and CAR-DQN with increasing perturbation budgets are shown in Table
7
.

Training Stability.
We also observe that there are some instability phenomena in the training of CAR, RADIAL, and WocaR in Figure
7
and
6
. We conjecture that the occasional instability in CAR training comes from the unified loss combining natural and robustness objectives which may cause undesirable optimization direction under a batch of special samples. The instability of RADIAL is particularly evident in the robustness curve on the BankHeist environment and natural curve on the Freeway environment and it may be from the larger batch size (=128) setting during the RADIAL training while CAR, SA-DQN and WocaR set the batch size as 32 or 16. The worst-case estimation of WocaR may be inaccurate in some states and WocaR also uses a small batch of size 16. The combination of these two can lead to instability, especially in complex environments such as RoadRunner and BankHeist. Another possible reason is that CAR, RADIAL, and WocaR all use the cheap relaxation method leading to a loose bound while SA-DQN utilizes a tighter relaxation.

Table 6:
Average episode rewards
\pm
standard error of the mean over 50 episodes on baselines and CAR-DQN on Atari games. The best results of the algorithm with the same type of solver are highlighted in bold. CAR-DQN with the PGD solver outperforms SA-DQN with the PGD solver in all metrics and achieves remarkably better robustness (
110\%
higher reward) on RoadRunner. CAR-DQN with the convex relaxation solver outperforms baselines in a majority of cases.

Figure 17:
Episode rewards of CAR-DQN with different batch sizes and learning rates during training on RoadRunner and BankHeist with and without PGD attack.

Insensitivity of Learning Rate and Batch Size.

We compare the performance of CAR-DQN with different small batch size
(16,32)
and learning rate
(1.25\times 10^{-4},6.25\times 10^{-5})
which are respectively used by
Zhang et al. 2020b
;
Liang et al. 2022
. As shown in Figure
17
, we can see CAR-DQN is insensitive to these parameters.

H.2
Comparisons and Analysis between RADIAL-DQN and CAR-DQN with Increasing Perturbation Radius

The core at RADIAL-DQN is a heuristic robust regularization that minimizes the overlap between bounds of perturbed Q values of the current action and others:

\mathcal{L}_{radial}\left(\theta\right)=\mathbb{E}_{\left(s,a,s^{\prime},r\right)}\left[\sum_{y}Q_{\text{diff}}(s,y)\cdot{Ovl}(s,y,\epsilon)\right],

where

Q_{\text{diff}}(s,y)=\max(0,Q(s,a)-Q(s,y)),\ Ovl(s,y,\epsilon)=\max(0,\bar{Q}(s,y,\epsilon)-\underline{Q}(s,a,\epsilon)+\eta)
and
\eta=c\cdot Q_{\text{diff}}(s,y),c=0.5
.
Q_{\text{diff}}
is treated as a constant for the optimization.
We consider RADIAL-DQN could perform better than CAR-DQN with increasing perturbation radius since
\mathcal{L}_{radial}\left(\theta\right)
is a stronger regularization to enhance robustness while compromising natural rewards. The stronger robust constraint is mainly reflected in two aspects:

•

The loose bounds. RADIAL-DQN uses the cheap but loose convex relaxation method (IBP) to estimate
\bar{Q}(s,y,\epsilon)
and
\underline{Q}(s,a,\epsilon)
.

•

The positive margin
\eta
.

They both result in
{Ovl}(s,y,\epsilon)
a stronger constraint for representing the overlap of perturbed Q values.
However, RADIAL-DQN has the following weaknesses:

•

\mathcal{L}_{radial}\left(\theta\right)
will harm natural rewards. As shown in Figure
7
, the natural rewards curve of RADIAL-DQN on RoadRunner distinctly tends to decrease, especially around 0.5 million steps. In contrast, the natural curves of our CAR-DQN showcase more stable upward trends in all environments. Besides, as shown in Table
10
, RADIAL-DQN training with a larger radius attains lower natural rewards which also restricts robustness according to our theory, while CAR-DQN keeps a better and consistent natural and robust performance.

•

Heuristic implementation lacks theoretical guarantees and introduces sensitive hyperparameter
c
. We conduct additional experiments on RoadRunner with different
c
and observe the sensitivity of RADIAL-DQN to the choice of
c
, as shown in Table
9
. Larger
c
could cause poor performance because the robustness constraint is too strict and thus the policy degrades to some simple policy with lower rewards. Smaller
c
may result in much weaker robustness. By contrast, our CAR-DQN is developed based on the theory of optimal robust policy and stability. Although we also introduce a hyperparameter
\lambda
, our ablation studies in Figure
9
show that our algorithm is insensitive to the soft coefficient
\lambda
.

Performance of RADIAL-DQN sensitive to different positive margins
c\cdot Q_{\text{diff}}(s,y)
.

)

3992\pm 482

3992\pm 482

3992\pm 482

3992\pm 482

3992\pm 482

3992\pm 482

•

Depending on the currently learned optimal action.
\mathcal{L}_{radial}\left(\theta\right)
essentially takes the currently learned action as a robust label which may produce a wrong direction for robustness training if the learned action is not optimal. In contrast, our CAR-DQN seeks optimal robust policies with theoretical guarantees and does not utilize the learned action for robustness training, simultaneously improving natural and robust performance.

The main motivation of CAR-DQN based on our theory is to improve natural and robust performance concurrently which makes sense in real-world scenarios where strong adversarial attacks are relatively rare.
Our training loss can guarantee robustness under the attack with the same perturbation radius as the training. We also think it is a very significant problem whether and how we can design an algorithm training with little epsilon and theoretically guarantee robustness for larger epsilon. However, this is beyond the scope of our paper and we will consider this problem in subsequent work.

Moreover, as shown in Table
7
, CAR-DQN also achieves the top performance in larger perturbation radiuses on Pong and BankHeist and matches the RADIAL-DQN on Freeway. To show the superiority of CAR-DQN further, we also train CAR-DQN with a perturbation radius of 3/255 and 5/255 on RoadRunner for 4.5 million steps (see Table
10
).

We can see that CAR-DQN still attains superior natural and robust performance training with larger attack radiuses while RADIAL-DQN markedly degrades its natural performance due to the too-strong robustness constraint.
